are trained on the racecourse, 'which has been , turned into a camp. Horses are also stabled there in great numbers, and sent on as re- quired. The favourite promenade is this course, where the soldiers offer all visitors afternoon tea,.

Brigadier Murray, of the Salvation Army. A distinguished member of the Salvation Army is Brigadier Murray, a. daughter of the late Lieut.-General Sir John Murray, whese reputation as a great soldier and a goad man was earned in India. He fought during the Indian Mutiny, when he raised and commanded a regiment. Lady Murray accompanied him thrOughout the pampaign, and on one occasion carried despatches a distance of seventy lanes. Miss Murray was in charge of the Salvation Army expedition during the Beer War., wizen she and her companions ministered to British and Boer alike. She is now with His Majegtes forces, and caring for soldiers and sailors. She sent home a short time ago a very graphic account of her experiences, when the German army entered Brussels.

Autumn Capes. The summer cape, which carried all before it, is not suited to autumn wear, and it has there- fore been very considerably modified. The vol- uminous shape open in front to show an elabor- ate blouse or bodice, has given place to a gar- ment bearing a strong resemblance to a shawl, and ending in a point at the back. The out- line of the shoulders is now visible, the heavy folds having disappeared. This cape is modelled on the brigand design, but is eminently femin- ine in its lines, and in its quaint style. If forms a part of many of the autumn suits, more espe- cially when these are of dark blue serge, a con- trasting waistcoat of some brightly-striped material giving the necessary note of relief. Fancy plush is often used in this connectiou, more especially in a rich tomato shade. This shade will be very popular, also a colour that resembles the lees of wine.

Military Modes. Dress has always been largely influenced by wars, and the present stupendous struggle, in which so many nations are engaged, is respon- sible for the introduction of d military element in novelties for this autumn. This is particu- larly noticeable in the new millinery. Vivid

Pigeon Pie. Cut up four pigeons into halves, and cut up hull a pound of ham into pieces. Stew these for one and a half hours. Season with pepper and salt. Place these in a, pie-dish, and cover with short pastry. Decorate the top, and glaze with white of egg. Bake in a moderately hot oven for an hour. Serve either hot or cold. The feet of the birds should be saved, scalded, and skinned, and placed in the top of the pie as a garnish.

• red or scarlet hats have appeared, and will doubtless have a vogue. They are small in shape, and are quite simply trimmed with flowers of the same colour and material. A new ' model, also in velvet, closely resembles the Glen- garry cap, worn by the brave Highland soldiers. Placed somewhat rakishly on one side of the bead, the coiffure is shown to a greater extent than hitherto. This cap has an exceedingly beautiful line and is very becoming to youthful features and outlines. When wora iiti a kilted tunic, especially if the latter be of a tar- tan plaid, the toilette is strongly suggestive of the .uniform of a Highland regiment.

EVOLUTION OF THE BANANA. Wild bananas are almost wholly seeds, but what pulp is found is sweet and agreeable. The original banana had little value as a fruit. 0. F. Cook, in an article in the 1903 Report of the Smithsonian Institution, expressed the opinion that it was first cultivated as a root crop, the roots being used even to-day by the natives of some regions, while the tender heart is still eaten in Abyssinia. The "Journal of Heredity" tells the story of the wild bananas, which are found in Borneo, the Philippine Islands, India, Africa, Ceylon, "on the rocky slopes of mountainous regions of the moist tropics." Odoardo Beocari went to Borneo to look for the original banana. But he considers that all the wild bananas to-day are forms that have escaped from cultivation in times past. For the banana has been a well-known cultivated fruit throughout historic times. , Beccari suggests that it was cultivated as far back as the Pliocene epoch. Originating in the Indo-Malayan region, it probably spread westward and eastward. It is commonly be- lieved that it was introduced into America by the Spaniards, though Cook and others main- tain that it is a native of America. The bananas we eat have only rudimentary seeds. The minute seeds they do contain would not grow if planted. The wild ones, as has been said, are little but seeds. The seedless banana is the result either of hybridisation or of improvement by a sexual reproduction, of which the potato is the best known example. Beginning to cultivate it as a root crop, primi- tive men discovered that their attention was improving its fruit. They naturally selected for cultivation those that bore the largest quantity of pulp and the smallest of seed. So, in the course of time, the banana as we know it to-day was developed.

The New Collar. A new collar, which will be built in fur later I on, is a combination of sailor and stand-up, with a V opening in front. It is made in one -piece, and can be easily tacked into a blouse, and as this part soils quickly the idea has ,orne practical value. Flat, turn-back cuffs are worn with it, and these are also detachable. Belts are taking the place of sashes on many dresses, more especially when blouses ate worn with different skirts. Broad Roman striped ribbon belts are fastened with three small, instead of one large, buckle, as the three prevent the belt from dropping in front, and a certain trimness at the waist is a feature of the season's modes. Shirts and blouses were never more simple in build, or more devoid of unnecessary trimming. Gold buttons are popular just at present, an- other sign of the military influence. Many blouses are touched with scarlet and black, and military braid, both wide and narrow, is largely used on garments of every description. Concernit' eadgear. There is a certain trimness about the new hats which has been lacking in millinery for some time. The new models axe intended to be placed firmly on the head, which they no longer cover. Gold is the most popular trimming, and a new galon is to have quite a vogue. Some of the black velvet models turn up quite impertinently at the side, where the brim is as wide as it is narrow on the other. A band of gold galon at the base of the crown is often the sole trimming, finished by a flat bow at the side. Gold gauze takes the place of tulle in transparent mounts. Even ostrich feathers are made to look as if they were gilded. Three cornered hats are a favourite shape in black velvet. These also pre trimmed with gold.

WHAT M.P.'S MAY NOT DO. Many are the privileges which a member oi the British House of Commons enjoys. At the same time, however, there are quite a number of unwritten, laws governing his conduct while in the House, which he must observe unless he wishes to bring upon himself nhenviable notice. Although the House of Comnfons, for instance, has been described as the best club in Europe, members must not play cards in the smoking - rooms, while there is no provision for billiards. Neither must a member smoke in the House itself or in the division lobbies or galleries, nor have food except in the dining-rooms, although there is no restriction on the beverages which may be partaken of during a speech. Again, in strict Parliamentary law, any mem- ber is allowed to occupy any seat in the Cham- ber that happens to be vacant. In practice, however, the front benches on either side of the Speaker are sacred to the Ministers of the Government, and the principal members of the Opposition respectively, and an ordinary mem- ber would no more think of insisting upon tak- ing a seat on either of these benches during a sitting of the House than he would of turning the Speaker out of his chair. And should a member on any particular occasion find him- self by accident seated amongst political oppon- ents, he must not rise to speak nor must he take part in applause. It is necessary, if he wishes to reserve a seat for the day, to place upon it a card found upon the table of the House before prayers, and re- turn at prayer time to clinch his claim. Talk- ing of prayers, it might be mentioned that the 67th Psalm is always read before the opening of each day's sitting, and when the actual prayers begin, members, who have been standing with their faces to the floor of the House, turn round and face the walls of the Chamber. Strangers, as " P.W.W." points out in his interesting little book, "Everybody's Guide to Parlia- ment," are not permitted to prayers, even the Press being locked out. Among other points of conduct which a mem- ber must observe is the rule which forbids the display in the Honse of any papers or books save those which concern the business of Par- liament for the time being. The rule against reading newspapers is strictly enforced, both on the floor of the House and in all the galleries. No member is supposed to enter the House in an overcoat or carrying an umbrella, and al- though fashion has changed in regard to head- gear since the days when a member who wore anything but a silk hat was regarded as eccen- tric, cloth caps are not permitted, despite Keir Hardie's attempt years ago to introduce that form of head-covering. A member who forgets when walking up the floor of the House, or crossing from one side to the other, to bow to the chair, is quickly re- minded of his shortcoming. It is a rule, how- ever, that a member must not cross between the chair and a member who is speaking. "This rule," says " P.W.W.," "is somewhat relaxed where the speaking member stands further back than the first two benches. But if from his seat a member is compelled to pass the intercepting line, he must, as it were, con- done the necessity by bowing very low. "On one occasion Colonel Seely aroused great protest by passing up the gangway, in front of a speaking member, in order to whisper to the late Sir Walter Foster. The explanation was that this eminent physician's services were in- stantly required for Sir Alfred Billson, who had been seized by a fatal illness in the division lobby. The Houses of Parliament being a royal palace, the body of a person dying within the precincts cannot be removed without the ap- proval of the King's coroner."

EXTENSION OF AREA UNDER WHEAT DESIRABLE.

The Agricultural Consultative Committee state that with the Arrival of the time for pre- paring the land for autumn and winter sowing, they desire again to impress upon farmers the great importance, in the national interest, that the acreage under wheat during the coming year should be largely increased. "It should not be forgotten that, on clean land, and by the aid of suitable artificial manure, good crops of Wheat can be obtained in succes- sive years. Attention is drawn to Section 26 of the Agricultural Holdings Act, 1908, which per- mits any system of cropping subject to the hold- ing being protected from deterioration. There is much land of a certain class now under grass which would probably pay for breaking up. If this land is scheduled as arable in the farm agreement, the tenant has the option of ploughing it up. If it is scheduled as grass, the Agricultural Consultative Committee sug- gests co-operation between owner and occupier tts to, the advisability of breaking up certain fields in view of the national question of in- creasing home-grown foodstuffs." The committee, of course, do not suggest that good grazing land should be broken up, and they realise that the circumstances of each in- dividual holding must be examined in consider- ing the above recommendations; but they point out that should it be found possible to increase 1 the wheat acreage without detriment to the live stock and general organisation of the par- ticular holding, there seems every reason to sup- pose that it might prove to the financial advan- tage of the grower.

At Sight. "So your daughter has married the titled stranger," said the romantic woman. "Yes." answered Mr. Cumrox, glancing up from his check book. "Was it a case of love at sight?" "Yes. Now it's a case of draw at sight."

Probably True. "George!" She looked into his eyes unques- tioningly. "George," she said, "it isn't for my money that you love me, is it?" He stroked the head resting there against his manly breast, and made reply: "My darling, no! Why, little one, I would love you if—if—if—you had ten times as much money." "Oh, George." And she sighed as though relieved of a great weight. •

Certainly New to the Business. She was newly married and did not know a little bit about either housekeeping or shop- ping. It was a crusher; but Gus, the grocer, was an experienced man and clever, so he kept writing and did not even smile. " I want ten pounds of paralysed sugar," she began with a businesslike air. " Yes'm. Anything else?" "Two tins of condemned milk." yes,m.." He set down pulverised sugar and condensed milk. "Anything more, ma'am?" "A bag of fresh salt. Be sure it's fresh." " Yes'm. What next?" "A pound of desecrated codfish." He glibly wrote " dessicated cod." "Nothing more, ma'am?"

"No." " We have some nice horseradish, just in." "It would be of no use to us," she said; "we do not keep a horse."

not, you have seen pictures of one. But do you know why they fly? For many, many years men have been trying to float in the air, and many lives have been lost in the attempt. Years ago a balloon, a silk bag filled with gas, was invented, and this held men up in the air because it was lighter than the air it rested upon. Hanging at the bottom of the balloon was a basket, in which men sat down. Many different kinds of balloons have been made, and to-day -we find them fitted with machinery to send them along, and with a rudder, just like a ship, to steer them. The biggest balloon ever made, with machin- ery to drive it, is called the "Zeppelin." This kind is used by the Germans. It is a very big thing, but can only carry about 20 mere A boat on the sea, built the same size, would carry about 1,000 people. This kind of airboat floats because it is lighter than air. The aeroplane is heavier than air and that floats also. How does it do it? Just like the bird does, and just like the kites the boys send up in the air. . The bird and the aeroplanes both have wings, which act in a similar manner. The boy's kite with the string tied to it, is forced upwards against the wind because it slopes a little with its face looking down towards the ground, and because the wind, blowing against it, pushes it up in the air. When the boy pulls the string the kite goes forward. That is how the aero- plane works, but, instead of somebody pulling it along with string, it has machinery which drives it. The aeroplane's wings catch the wind on which it sails and this holds it up.

THE HONEST MAN. Long, long ago a certain king wanted a man to 7-.. look after his money and his great treasures. "The man I am looking for must be very honest and not take anything which belongs to me," said his majesty. It was hard to find such a man. The king could not advertise for one, because newspapers and printing were unknown, so he sent five of his wise men all over the country, but each came back with the message that not a single honest man could he found. The king was very angry about this. He did not know what to do. One morning, when he looked more troubled than usual, the queen said she could find an honest man if the king would Promise to buy her a valuable string of beads. lie promised and on an appointed day twenty men came forward. The king and queen sat side by side with all their attendants, when the men entered the royal chamber. "I want you all to dance," said he queen, "to show to his majesty how grace- ful you are." Only one man did as he was asked, and the queen told the king that that was the man he wanted. The king was angry again, and said he wanted an honest man and not one who only did as he was told. CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

The Queen and the War. Parliament being in recess and the war tak- ing a favourable course, the Court will shortly remove to Windsor, but the Queen will continue to give her active assistance to the many schemes for relieving hardships and privation occasioned by the war. An instant response hits been made to her Majesty's appeal for woollen socks and belts for the soldiers at the front, and with the perceptible advance of winter it is necessary for us all to do what we can to help our troops to face the disc,omforts which it brings in its train. The Queen has also greatly interested herself, in the scheme for the employ- ment of women, and arrangements have been made whereby work will be provided at thirty factories near London. The uniform rate of payment will be threepence per hour for a forty- four hour week. Later on the same arrange- ments will be made with factories in the pro- vinces. Her Majesty has ordered a number of warm useful garments, which she usually makes herself and distributes at Christmas among the tenants of the Royal estates.

Our Troops in France. The towns in the South and West of France, including Tours and Bordeaux, are filled to over- flowing with refugees from Northern France, Paris and Belgium. Many French people, well known in English society, have sought sanctuary in London, which is also fuller than is usually the case at this time of year. There are a num- ber of British people at Nantes, which may be termed a base for the British troops in France. The residents greet each trainload with every mark of sympathy, and shower flowers, fruits— such as pears, apples and peaches—and choco- lates upon the soldiers, and when a regiment leaves for the front, the same attentions are shown by French and British of all classes. Our soldiers make a very brief halt, but recruits

Bottling Fruit. Choose wide-mouthed bottles and clean them thoroughly. Pick just ripe fruit, clean and dry. Place in bottles. Apples and pears should be sliced in quarters, plums and damsons put in whole. Fill the bottles with clean water. Stand them up to their necks in water in a fish-kettle or open boiler. The bottles MIIBT be open. Slowly raise the water to the boil and let it boil for twenty minutes or half an hour. Take out the bottles singly and without the smallest delay tie the mouths with clean bladder whilst the water in the bottles is at boiling heat. If corks are used instead of bladders scald them first. Insert them and seal the tops with seal- ing or bottle wax. Remember to boil the water gradually. the bottles in any cool plate. Make as much jam as possible, using smaller quantities of sugar if price is high. Blackberries can be preserved with a smaller quantity of sugar than other fruits. The Preserving of Beans. Secure earthen jars with wide mouth. Clean thoroughly with boiling water. Pick the beans when dry or dry them before the fire. Wipe them with a cloth. Tear away the strings and slice them as for cooking. Sprinkle layer of clean kitchen salt at bottom of jar, put in a layer of beans, then more salt, and so on until the jar is full and heaped up. Cover jar with clean plate on which a weight is placed. Next day remove any dirt on top of jar, add more beans and salt till jar is full. Cover mouth with brown paper tied down. Once or twice in the next ten days uncover and remove any scum. Finally cover with bladder tied down. To prepare for eating take out the beans and soak thoroughly in several changes of water until free froth salt. Boil for rather a shorter time than for fresh

Store

beans. To increase their food value strain off the water when the beans have boiled soft, add a piece of dripping or butter to the pot, stir for five or ten minutes and dish out. Fruit Drying in Brick Ovens. Though a somewhat tedious process, drying of apples, pears and plums is well worth doing by those who have brick ovens. To dry the fruit:— (1) Apples and pears should be quartered. (2) Plums left whole. A layer of fruit should be placed on the lid of a wicker basket which should be put in an oven

MT DEAR CHILDREN,— This week I have pleasure in announc- ing the winner of the prize in the competition essay "Why I should be loyal." The winner

MAGGIE RANDLES, 329, Wellfield-street, Warrington. Maggie's essay was not the best sent in, but most other competitors did not keep to the sub, ject we set them. We asked them to say "WHY I should be loyal," and they wrote upon "HOW I can be loyal." One of the most important things in this world is accuracy, and we cannot ,answer a question accurately if we don't make - sure that we understand the question before we answer it. So that Maggie takes the prize in preference ; to those who wrote better essays, because she did what she was told to do. You will find as you grow older that carefulness and thought- fulness win the substantial prizes of life oftener, 14, perhaps, than there brilliance. Here we have a little object lesson which I hope will be of lasting value to all my nephews and nieces. Maggie thinks that as England is free and well governed there is every reason why she, an Eng- lish girl, should be loyal; which is really the reason why we should all be loyal. If we are all true to ourselves we shall have no fear of Ger- mans interfering with us and our affairs. We are free and intend to remain so. I hope the unsuccessful competitors will try again for the next prize, and, keeping to the text, they will have a better chance of winning. I very often wonder what kind of books you like to read. If you like reading you should join the library, for there you can borrow all kinds of good books —and good books are the only ones worth read-

HINTS ON HEALTH. Useful Information in Popular

Language.

Pains in the Joints. .Heart disease is one of the most serious con- ditions from which a child can suffer, and when due to disease of the heart valves, is incurable, and renders the sufferer a permanent invalid. It cannot be too often repeated, nor too carefully borne in mind, that any child who complains of pains in its joints has almost certainly got acute rheumatism, and the heart is in grave danger of suffering irreparable damage. Such a child may appear to ail but little, yet its temperature ought to be taken at once, and, if raised in the slightest degree, the child should be sent to bed and the doctor called in. Neglect to do so is responsible for most of those ex- tremely sad cases of heart disease in young children with all its prolonged suffering and premature death.—Dr. Medley Marsh.

Hot Food. Food should be served up, says Dr. Toogood, somewhere about the temperature of the body, between 98 and 100 degrees. It is all right if cold, but if neither hot nor cold it IS very dis- tasteful. He is certain that tea :s yery often taken much too hot by women. Some people take it almost boiling, which is very harmful, as the tissues of the body are not able to stand it. Rest and exercise should be taken before and after meals. It is not advisable for the adult to sleep after a meal. Rest certainly, but not sleep, because the work of the stomach should be largely completed before retiring.

Warts. To remove warts apply strong soda and water for a few days and then paint them with aethereal tincture of tannin. Or, having covered the skin around the wart thickly with lard, ap- ply over the surface of the growth one or two drops of strong hydrochloric or nitric acid; then keep the part covered up until the scab separ- ates. We do not recommend this treatment and advise you to have the warts removed by a doctor.

Hints on the Baby's Food. A regular hour for feeding the baby, is indis- pensable, as feeding it every time it cries is not only bad as a habit, but in time will ruin the child's digestion. Two nursing bottles are necessary, so that they may be kept exquisitely clean; hot water and soap are the best clean- sers. When being fed the baby should be taken from its cradle. It is a bad habit to leave the bottle in bed with a child, as it is apt to fall asleep, and upon waking drink part of the milk cold. Any milk remaining in the bottle should not be warmed up, but fresh milk given each time.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0012.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GROWING OF LINSEED FOR FEEDING PURPOSES.

In order that the dairy farmer may produce milk most economically, and realise the maxi- mum return on invested capital, it is imperative that he should (amongst other things) maintain a deep milking strain of cows. As a means to this end the rearing of the heifer calves from his best animals id all-important. Furthermore, is desirable that as large a number as possible of calves of the beef tylie should be reared for fattening purposes. In view of the great and ever-increasing demand for milk, and its conse- quent high price, its use as an economical food for calves is an impossibility. Skim milk may be employed, but a substitute for the fat is necessary. Linseed as a source of easily diges- tible fat at once suggests itself, but at current winter prices it is an expensive food. The ques- tion therefore arises—can the English farmer ' produce linseed at less cost than he can buy it? From experiments which have been conducted, it would appear that this question can be answered in the affirmative. Indeed, there is evidence to show that it may be grown profitably for sale. How this may be done is fully explained in leaflet No. 278, which may be obtained free of charge and post free on application to the Secre- tary, Board of Agriculture and Fisheries, 4, Whitehall-place, London, S.W. Letters of ap- plication so addressed need not be stamped.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0013.txt | included=true | included =====
UTILISATION OF CEREAL OFFALS.

- • • -- - -- The Board of Agriculture also draws attention (in special leaflet No. 8) to a number of useful feeding stuffs, some of which have hitherto been largely exported, but which will in all prob- ability now be obtainable in this country at relatively low prices. The feeding stuffs dealt with in this leaflet are :—Wheat bran, sharps and middlings, dried grains, dried yeast, gluten meal and gluten feed, Soya bean cake and meal, cocoanut cake, palmnut cake, and fish meal.

WEATHER AND MILK PRODUCTION. The effect of weather on milk production is worth studying. The extremely sensitive nature of dairy cows is well known to those concerned, and those ignorant of these mysteries may gather the same from the expression of a cow's eye—a faithful recorder of bovine temperament. It is not perhaps generally known to what an extent the very delicate lacteal organs are affected by weather, but some interesting records have shown the chilling and immediate effect of cold wind on the cow's capacity for milk-mak- ing. It is well known, too, that cows can re- tain their milk at will when they deem it well to do so, either from fear or caution when a calf is concerned.—" Agricultural Economist and Horticultural Review."

POTATO HARVESTING.

There are three ways of lifting potatoes, says "Farm and Home." The most common method is to raise them by the fork, a small piece of the ridge being thrown out, and the potatoes then gathered by women or boys. We prefer this method to any other, as the potatoes are easy to gather, and the digging forms a good preparation of the soil for the succeeding crop. An ordinary plough working as deep as the tubers is sometimes used; this method only par- tially uncovers the potatoes, and the services of the man with the fork have to be requisitioned. The various potato-digging machines throw out the potatoes, which simply require gathering and separating into good and bad. They have been improved of late years, and may now be re- girded as useful helps where there is a large area under the crop. The picking should always be put out by the piece, at so much per sack, M i for s job s easily scamped unless very care- fully supervised. After pickil the field should

===== 0002601_19141003_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
• -••.- --

BARLESS FIRE GRATES. Wood Chimney Pieces, Kitchen Ranges, Tile Panels, New and Artistic Examples now showing.

be harrowed and all missed potatoes gathered up. Harvesting must be done only when both the soil and weather are dry, otherwise both the land and crop suffer. After the digging the potatoes should be left for an hour or so before gathering, but on no account must they be left out overnight, for if raik or frost comes on the tubers will soon become rotten. In handling potatoes great care is necessary, for if bruised or knocked about they never keep s 6 well.

FARM LABOUR.

Any farmer who is experiencing difficulty in obtaining farm hands is recommended to apply to the local Labour Exchange, the address of which can be obtained at any Post Office. The Labour Exchanges are making special efforts to furnish farmers with the means of suitable men and women who have had previous experience in farm work, and all applicants will be inter- viewed and passed by a farmer of standing before they are put into communication with farmers who are in need of them. In notifying their requirements, farmers should state fully the wages and other terms they are prepared to offer, and whether free board and lodging will be provided. They should also state clearly whether the work to be done is unskilled or re- quires special qualifications; whether it can be done by women, and if so, whether suitable accommodation is available for them. If a labourer is sent from a distance his rail- way fare iartay be advanced through the Labour Exchange, subject to an undertaking either on his part or on the part of the farmer, to repay the amount so advanced. If desired, arrange- ments may be made with the labourer, subject to the consent of the farmer, whereby the latter will deduct the sums advanced from the wages and remit the same to the Labour Exchange. Farmers are invited to support a movement which has been started for the purpose of assist- ing them to maintain an adequate supply of labour. •

===== 0002601_19141003_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

PROTECT THE APPLE TREES. • In visiting gardens, how often do we see that destructive pest, American blight, infesting apple trees. One of the best remedies is a mix- ture known and sold as Woburn wash, which should be freely used wherever this bane of the fruit grower is present. The stems and every portion of the branches on which the American blight is found should be painted over with this, using a half-worn painter's sash tool for the purpose; all the twigs and smaller branches should be sprayed with it. The soil, too, be- neath the trees should be cleared away, and then spray the trees thoroughly. The person who is doing the work should be careful to use gloves, and don some old clothes, as this Woburn wash contains caustic soda. It can be purchased from any horticultural sundriesman ready for dilution. One dressing will probably not be sufficient, and next season, on the first appear- ance of the pest, at once destroy any stray ones that may be found, by brushing the parts of the tree where found with methylated spirit or un- diluted paraffin. A friend of mine, says a writer in " Farm and Home," in the Eastern Counties uses neat's foot oil. This is slightly warmed, and the affected shoots are painted with this.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
HARDY ANNUALS FROM AUTUMN-SOWN

_ SEEDS. Those who have never grown hardy annuals from autumn-sown seed have quite failed to realise their worth when so treated. There is no comparison between plants that have been ' sown in the spring and those that have gathered strength, and obtained a firm root-hold from autumn-sown seed. Many do not succeed with autumn-sown annuals, simply because they de- lay sowing until it is too late. This is more ap- parent when the soil is heavy and laden with moisture, therefore cold, and preventing the free growth of the seedlings and their establish- ment before the winter. Such plants, even if they do not perish, are weakly and devoid of the vigour necessary to a full flowering in the com- ing spring. The ground now being warm and fairly moist, seeds may even be sown now with every chance of success.

PRESENT HEAVY RESPONSIBILITIES. A Big Wage Bill. . _

As regards the future, I cannot do better than read the clause in the report, both as to the advantages we are now reaping from new work of the Partington Steel and Iron Co., Ltd., and also the trouble we have to face in consequence of the reduction of labour due to the war and general dislocation of trade : " The position and prospect of this section of the company's business have, however, under- gone a remarkable change since the war broke out, and the sudden cessation of German com- petition has already proved beneficial in many directions. Some loss and great inconvenience was at first experienced by the total failure, without any notice, of all foreign supplies of raw material, which were largely in use at the works, but, fortunately, this company had 'at its disposal the ample resources of the allied works of the Partington Steel and Iron Co., Ltd., now in full operation, otherwise at least one-half of the ironworks and wire mills would have been closed down for an indefinite period. The general dislocation of trade and the scaileity of labour due to the war are at the present moment causes of much anxiety and trouble, and it is imuossible to forecast the immediate future." Yoar directors have a heavy duty to perform this year. In the first place, we have to keep our works going for the good of our employes, and when I tell you that the total number of men employed last year was 10,465, and our wages paid £772,355, you will understand what responsibility rests upon us to do our uest in continuing work. (Hear, hear.)

HELP FOR .SOLDIER-EMPLOYES.

We feel that it is our duty to assistilwives and families of those of the company's workmen in all our departments, who have joined Lord Kitchener's Army, the Territorials and Reservists. We are setting aside, as you Will observe by the resolution which is to be pr0.t5,000 .t5,000 for that purpose, and our men pro- posed, have agreed to join us and to make a levy on their wages to supplement this fund. We are very grateful to our men for doing this, as it will mean that those who are now fighting or preparing to fight during the war, will feel that their wives and families are provided with some- thing more than the allowance paid by Govern- ment. (Applause.) We are also forming committees of our own men to assist in the distribution of the fund, in order that such distribution may be made with as much sympathy and generosity as is possible to all requiring it. And finally we have the duty of endeavouring to make sufficient profits to pay a moderate dividend to our shareholders. (Hear, hear.) The thrifty who have saved, and perhaps having re- tired from active work from age or other causes, and are now dependent on the income from their savings, will feel the pinch of this war. It is _ _ _

_ impossible that profits should remain every- where as in a time of peace, and therefore, when it is possible, with every consideration of good finance and sufficient reserves to make a distri- bution of dividend, your directors consider that it is their duty to endeavour to do so, and they will work hard for this purpose. I beg to move the adoption of the report and statement of accounts. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19141003_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
TO CAPTURE GERMAN IRON

TRADE. Local Output May Double.

Mr. J. J. BLECKLY, seconding, agreed that this was a time for deeds rather than words, but it was necessary to draw attention to one or two matters that were apt to be overlooked. _ _

A common cry, he said, is that trade has gone to the dogs, and has been irrevocably ruined all over the world and will never recover. At the present moment, of course, all is chaos—the. great bulk of the export trade in iron and steel of all kinds cannot actually at the moment be worked at all; advanced first costs, impossible conditions of exchange, war risks and war freights so enormously enhance prices to the consumer abroad—who in many eases is hampered by old contracts that he cannot fulfil —that absolute collapse is the result, and ex- port merchants on this.eide cannot safely operate at all. This state of things must of necessity continue for some time to come, and in fact until the probable course of the war can be forecasted with some degree of certainty. When that time conies, presuming, as we are bound to do, that the British Fleet continues to hold the sea and that our troops are billeted in Berlin, what are the prospects for the British iron trade? The whole export trade of the world may be estimated at 14,000,000 tons; Germany and Bel- gium appropriate 7,000,000 tons, Great Britain 5,000,000 tons, and America 2,000,000 tons. The probable and almost certain result of the Kaiser's action will be, on a reasonable esti- mate, to reduce Germany's exports of iron and steel by at least one-half, or by 3,000,000 tons, which will give Great Britain and America the opportunity of increasing their export by some- thing like 50 per cent. The result of such a change it is impossible to calculate, but it must add enormously to their prosperity. (Hear, hear.)

But to come a little nearer home—the actual imports into Great Britain itself, mainly from Germany, of the commodities made in the com- pany's own works, and of which they are the largest producers in this country, that is to say, iron bars, hoops and strips, wire rods and finished wire, were last year 421,000 tons, or three times the whole production of the Pearson and Knowles works, which totalled 140,000 tons of these specialities. If these imports into our home market should fall off even 50 per cent.— and they ought properly to cease altogether— the additional demand on Pearson and Knowles for home trade alone might easily be doubled to the great advantage of their workmen and their shareholders. (Hear, hear.) This process would not mean capturing German trade, but resuming our own old trades filched from us by German interlopers, not by fair competition and superior methods, but by the aid of export boun- ties and subsidised steamships, which have ruined much of our business. This I am con- vinced is a sober estimate of what is before us, and almost certain to happen so soon as the German dumping incubus is removed and we have a free hand. I remember the 1870 war and what resulted from it—the effect of this war must be incalculably greater. (Applause.) The resolution was carried unanimously.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. RYLANDS AND PRESENT ANXIETIES.

Spring Prospects.

The CommAx moved: "That the usual divi- dend at the rate of 6 per cent. per annum on the first and second preference shares, and a divi- dend at the rate of 5 per cent, per annum on the ordinary shares—all less tax—for the half-year ending 30th June last, be paid forthwith," Mr. W. PETER RYLANDS, in seconding, said The present moment is one of very great diffi- culty and anxiety. We, as mentioned in the re- port, Wave been in the habit of getting large supplies of raw material from the Continent, and upon them we made contracts for a supply of finished wire and other goods. The interrup- tion of these supplies naturally lands us in the unfortunate position of having commitments for which we have no cover. Then we have a large number of our men going to join the colours. We are pleased that it should be so. In Rylands Bros. we have lost something like 300 or more of our men, and they are still going. This is, of course, very satisfactory from a national point of view, but their places are hard to fill. The enlistment all over the country has created a great shortage in the labour market, and it is only with great difficulty we have gradually found men to take the places of those who have left us. Then, again, we get fur-

===== 0002601_19141003_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, OCTOBER 3, 1914.

ther loss because it naturally takes some time before the men we do get obtain the experience that is necessary for efficient work. There is also the interruption of the export trade, upon which we are so dependent. At Bylands Bros. about half of our trade is for ex- port, and owing to the causes referred to—the difficulties of freight, the war risks and high prices—the flow of the export trade has been checked altogether. But while that is the posi- tion—and it is a very difficult one—we must reflect that the world's commerce has to be car- ried on. The consumers in our colonies want their wiw, iron and galvanised roofs as much now as ever, and this interference with the flow of trade is likely to result in an extra flow of trade when the dammed up trade gets released— I (hear, hear)—and when commerce gets into a more normal condition. And so it is that, while the present is a time full of anxiety, I personally hold the view that if the war proceeds, as we may hope, in a satisfactory manner from our point of view, that the world's commerce may be expected to get into a more normal condition during the next few months, and that as a result in the spring we ought to see a consider- able increase in the activity of our trade. (Hear, hear.) For the moment, as I have said, the time is one of difficulty and anxiety, and what we as directors are most immediately con- cerned with is to keep the works going and to give as much employment as we can to the men left at home. (Hear, hear, and applause.) The resolution was carried.

SOLDIERS' DEPENDANTS.

The CHAIRMAN submitted the following resolu- tion: "That the sum of £5,000 be placed at the disposal of the directors to be used at their discretion for the purpose of assisting the wives and families of those of this company's work- men who have joined Kitchener's Army and the Territorials and Reservists." Sir Harmood added that there was nothing they would do that day that would give them greater pleasure than to ,pass the resolution he had just sub- mitted. They had as a nation a very terrible enemy to face, an enemy whose methods in countries on the Continent showed what would be done in this country if, unhappily, Germany was successful in the war, and came across to these shores. Therefore every sympathy and every aid they could offer ,should be offered to those workmen who were leaving trade and doing a greater and a nobler work on the Con- tinent. (Hear, hear, and applause.) Mr. JOHN J. BLECRLY seconded, and the pro- position was carried.

MR. ASQUITH IN DUBLIN.

Great Irish Recruiting Meeting.

" You will remember your great reception in Dublin when you came to promise the Home Rule Bill. It will be nothing to the reception which you will get when you come after the Bill is passed into law."

These were the words reported to have been addressed by the Lord Mayor of Dublin to the Premier, when the latter suggested speaking in the four capitals of the United Kingdom. The prediction was fulfilled when Mr. Asquith visited Dublin on Friday. Mr. Asquith spoke from a platform which contained the leaders of the Nationalist Parlia- mentary party, the Nationalist Lord Mayor of Dublin, his Majesty's Lieutenant for the city and county, and delegates from the committee of the Irish Unionists' Alliance. Among the earliest arrivals were Lord Ashbourne and Mr. T. W. Russell, Mr. Swift• Mac Neill, General Hill, General Grenfell, Mr. John O'Connor, Lord Desert, Professor Mahaffy, and the High Sheriff of Dublin.

Nationalist Volunteers in uniform assisted the stewards during the time of waiting for the principal speakers, and rockets were fired frogt the roof. When the Lord Mayor (Mr. Coun- cillor Sherlock) was observed leading the proces- sion from the Mayor's parlour, the whole audi- ence rose, and broke into a tremendous round of cheers. The procession comprised in order Lady Aberdeen, Lord Aberdeen, Mrs. Asquith, Mr. Asquith, Miss Asquith,. Mr. Birrell, Mr. McKenna, Lord Crewe, Lord Pirrie, Mr. Red- mond, Mr. Devlin and Mr. T. Lough. An enthusiastic meeting followed, Mr. Asquith making one of his finest speeches, and the audi- ence showing unmistakably that Ireland stands with the Government in the defence of the rights of small nations.

Mr. Asquith announced that the first contin- gent of Indian troops landed at Marseilles the day before.

AID FOR BELGIUM.

Loan Without Interest.

Mr. Lloyd George, speaking at Criceieth last week-end, said: Last Wednesday the Belgian Chancellor came to see me. We had business to transact. There was no country more per- severing, industrious, and thrifty than Belgium. To this was their wealth due, but now they have not money to buy bullets. France and we agreed to find them the money—France 10 millions and we another 10. I went to the market on a certain Wednesday to secure sov- ereigns for Belgium, and instead of 10 millions I was readily offered 40, and the Bank was wil- ling to let me have even more. The loan will be without interest. (Cheers.) The Belgian Chancellor thanked me, but it is we who are under a debt of gratitude to his brave little country (Cheers.) You will be surprised to know that for every one Belgian soldier lost on the battlefield three unoffending innocent people were ruthlessly killed in that country.

FIGHTInG TYE WYITE PLAGUE.

New Use Suggested for Sankey Sanatorium.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
FIFTY BEDS FOB CONSUMPTIVES.

Nearly 400 Cases of Tuberculosis in the Town.

There are at present close upon four hundred persons in the Borough of Warrington suffering from that fell disease consumption, in its varying forms, and proper sanatorium accom- modation for the more serious of the cases has been a long-felt need. Temporarily, use has been made of the Smallpox Hospital at Sankey, but for several reasons, not the least of which is that it is not big enough, the accommoda- tion afforded by that building has been found unsatisfactory, and, accordingly, application has been made to the Local Government Board for their sanction to a loan of ..t4,i65 for the extension of the building, so that it shall be capable of affording beds for fifty patients instead of twenty-two and shall be permanently adapted to the purpose of fighting the deadly White Plague, which, annually, claims its thousands of pain-racked victims in this coun- try. Situated in an isolated position, the building at Sankey was admirably/ adapted for coping with smallpox in epidemic form, but it has been long felt by the public authority to whom such matters are entrusted that the more crying need was a sanatorium in which con- sumptives could be treated, for consumption, in its varying forms, is a species of infection that a community always has with it, whilst outbreaks of smallpox are sporadic. At the same time, of course, provision must be made to cope with so dire an epidemic disease, should it unhappily visit the town, and it is therefore Proposed by the Town Council, subject to the consent of the Local Government Board, to utilise a site on the Pear Tree Farm, adjoining the north-eastern boundary of Orford Park, and belonging to the town, for the purpose of putting up a new building in which to treat such smallpox cases as may occur. Sanction to carry out the proposed altera- tions was sought from the Local Government Board, and on Tuesday they sent down one of their Inspectors, Mr. Miles B. Arnold, to hold a public inquiry into the matter. The inquiry was held in the Town Hall, and was attended, in addition to the Inspec- tor, by Mr. A. T. Hallaway (deputy town clerk), Mr. J. Fairhurst (borough treasurer), Aldermen Evans and Smethurst, Councillors Pemberton and Gough (Warrington Corpora- tion), Mr. J. C. Sutton (clerk), Mr. B. S. New- combe (surveyor) and Mr. F. Kerfoot (inspec- tor) for the Rural District Council. The applications were taken separately, that for the loan being dealt with first.

SANKEY AIR GOOD FOR CONSUMPTIVES. The Deputy Town Clerk said the first portion of the application had to do with extensions in the building that was originally erected for the Smallpox Hospital at Sankey. As the Inspector was no doubt aware, the present building was opened in 1903 as a Smallpox Hospital, and it was used as such, occasionally, up to 1907, when the Corporation utilised it for a short time in treating a few cases--say, five or six in the first year—of tuberculosis. The Corporation then found that the treatment was so successful and that the air at . Sankey seemed so peculiarly fitted for the purposes of that treatment that they decided to extend the building and make it more useful. In 1911, having obtained the sanction of the Local Gov- ernment Board, the Corporation used the whole of the premises as a sanatorium, with the result that Warrington was one of the few towns in the country who were able to give sanatorium benefit when the Insurance Act first came into operation. It was unnecessary for him to point out the great assistance and help that that had been to the community as a whole, but they now found that the accom- modation at Sankey was not sufficient for the requirements of the town. In the old Small- pox Hospital they were not able to accommo- date more than 22 persons at once, and during the last year 76 people were treated, which meant that the beds were full, persons occupy- ing them for periods of three or four months, and during the whole time there were at least 30 or 35 persons on the "waiting list," who should have been taken into some sanatorium and treated at once. He thought that made it abundantly clear that at the present time the accommodation was not sufficient for the num- ber of cases there were in Warrington.

tution to be used for the treatment of persons inside and outside the town? MANY UNINSURED PATIENTS.

The Medical Officer.: Inside the town, for the ratepayers of this borough. I may say that almost half the cases of tuberculosis are uninsured and cannot be treated by the Insur- ance Committee. .

Councillor Gough : What accommodation would meet the requirements of the borough for smallpox cases? I would like to call your attention to a report that was given to the Council in which it was stated that the borough would be required by the Local Government Board to supply a smallpox hospital in lieu of the Sankey Sanatorium, and that the Local Government Board would be satisfied with a small inexpensive building. Bigger Sanatorium Necessary.

- - They had 374 known cases at the present time in the borough, all of them needing treat- ment. Of course, it would be quite possible to treat twice the number of cases if they kept patients in the sanatorium half the length of time, but he might point out that their con- tention was that the cases they had treated at the present time could not be kept in the length of time that was necessary, and that if they were to give adequate treatment it was absolutely essential that the hospital should be extended to the minimum at least of 50 beds. That was what they proposed to do. After detailing the various alterations pro- posed, Mr. Hallaway said the estimated cost was £4,765, as against .t 5,055 which was the figure originally submitted to the Board. The Inspector: The Surveyor thinks that will not be exceeded? Mr. Hallaway : That is the estimate of the Surveyor, and it is not expected to be exceeded. I would draw your attention to a letter of May 4, in which we asked for ,Z,055 for the alterations for the Sanatorium and £1,200 for a Smallpox Hospital. The Corporation do not, however, propose to proceed with the inquiry in so far as it affects the proposed Smallpox Hospital. The estimate of £4,765 reduces the amount applied for by about .t2BO. The Medical Officer said the alterations were necessary because they treated all cases of the disease, and they wanted each case sep- arated. Under the present conditions they had frequently to discharge cases which they would have liked to have kept. In 1913 they treated 76 cases, with 22 beds, and upon that basis, with 50 beds, they would be able to treat 175 cases. There were about 374 persons in the town suffering from pulmonary tuber- culosis.

The Medical Officer They are quite different diseases we are treating. Councillor Gough: Do you think that is suffi- cient reason for the change?

EXTREMELY GOOD RESULTS.

The Medical Officer: I do, from the results of treatment out there. They have been ex- tremely good, and I do not think we could get a better site anywhere in the neighbourhood for the treatment of these cases. That has been proved this last five or six years. Councillor Gough: Was this place not suc- cessful in treating smallpox cases? The Medical Officer: There has not been such an amount of smallpox as to necessitate a place like this. A SENTIMENTAL OBJECTION.

SCHOOL SWIMMING SPORTS.

Secondary Scholars Compete at

Annual Gala.

The Warrington Baths were the venue of a large number of people on Wednesday afternoon, on the occasion of the Secondary School's annual swimming gala. ' There were many entries for the various races, and the keenest competition was manifest throughout the events. The or- ganisation of the gala was in the capable hands of Miss Clarke and Mr. J. B. Baxter, members of the school staff, and Mr. A. Barlow, superin- tendent of the baths, arranged for the use of that institution.

At the conclusion of the races Mrs. J. S. Broome gracefully presented the prizes to the successful competitors, Rhoda Grant, the girl champion, first handing her a beautiful bouquet on behalf of the staff and scholars. Mr. E. S. A. Robson, the head master, presided, and, after the presentations, said that on behalf of the school he would like to thank all who had con- tributed to the success of the function—Mrs. Broome for coming to present the prizes, and also for providing some of them; Mr. G. W. A. Brown and Mr. T. Roberts for supplying other prizes; Miss Clarke and Mr. Baxter for organis- ing the gala; and Mr. Barlow for the use of the baths. He heartily congratulated the competi- tors—they had raised their standard since last year, and he hoped it would be raised still higher by the next gala. (Applause.) The motion was carried with acclamation, and, replying on behalf of his wife, Mr. J. S. Broome, who was received with loud applause, said it had been a pleasure to his wife to come and present the prizes that afternoon, and she had asked him to thank them very heartily for the hand- some bouquet they had given her. Already she felt that she knew many of the boys and girls of the school. (Applause.)

Mr. Broome then called for three cheers for the school, which were heartily given.

THE PRIZE-WINNERS. The following is a list of the prize-winners : Girls.

Champions-1, Rhoda Grant; 2, Muriel Criteh- ley.

HOW FARMERS MAY HELP.

At a meeting of the Chester Farmers' Club on Saturday a letter was read from the War Office appealing to members who possess suitable horses to offer them to the Deputy Assistant- Director of Remounts, Chester, and also sug- gesting that gifts of fresh fruit and vegetables, eggs and butter might be sent for the sick and wounded to the Western Territorial Hospitals at Liverpool and Manchester. It was decided to circularise the members on the subject.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
PEACE TREATY.

The United States Senate has ratified the treaties between the United States and Great Britain, France and Spain, providing that a period of a year shall be allowed for the investi- gation by a Commission of any question at issue before the outbreak of hostilities between the contracting parties.

BLESSINGS IN EVERY FAMILY

FREED FROM STATE FETTERS.

Advantages that will Accrue from Welsh Disestablishment.

ANGLICAN CLERIC'S OPINION.

Many matters affecting the faith of that Anglican community were touched upon at a meeting of the Warrington branch of the Eng- lish Church -Union, held in Batten Hall, Win- marleigh-street on Monday evening, when a small audience made up for what it lacked in num- bers by the interest it displayed in an instruc- tive address given by the Rev. E. Davies, vicar of St. Mark's, Low Moor, and in brief speeches by others interested in the work of the Union, which during recent years has played an im- portant part in shaping the destinies of the Church of England, and figured by no means in the background during the comparatively recent Parliamentary battle over the Welsh Disestab- lishment Bill. The Rev. W. J. Stanton, who is president of the local auxiliary, occupied the chair, and briefly introduced the principal Weaker, who, he reminded those present, was no stranger to Warrington, having occupied a pulpit in the town last June, on the occasion of a church anniversary, whilst he was also the district secretary to the Union.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
TREATY-BREAKING AND SACRILEGIOUS GERMANS.

Without further ado, the Rev. Davies com- menced his review of the work of the Union, entitling his address "The E.C.U. and current Church problems." He began by saying that these were upsetting times; the war had upset everything. Nevertheless, the war was, he felt, proving to be the means of bringing before the people many serious problems and, perhaps, it would have the result of awakening the English people to neglected truths and problems con- nected with the Church. There was, for in- stance, the matter of education—a very old pro- blem and one that was becoming, and would be- come, more and more, a very real problem. He ventured to think that it would again be very prominently brought before the English people, through the war and all that was connected with it. He supposed it had struck them all how very strange it was that the chief enemy— the Germans—who prided themselves on being the most cultured race on the face of the earth, should not only have made war without any seeming cause, but should also introduce a novel element into it—the breaking of treaties and the unnecessary destruction of sacred property. When they searched for the cause of that, he thought they would find that it rested in the system of education which had been in operation in Germany for the last thirty or forty years. Religion seemed to have been almost put into a secondary place; not merely denominational re- ligion, but religion altogether. The war would be a sort of appeal to the people of this coun- try, showing, as it did, on a very large scale, the effect of neglecting the true religious educa- tion of the young, a thing the E.C.U. had always fought for. The effect of putting ethics in thA place of religion explained much of what had happened in Germany. Ethics apart from re- ligion were mere human things. He was quite certain that one effect of the war would be the awakening of the conscience of England to the realisation that she must go back to foundation work and once again grasp the necessity for edu- cating the children on right religious lines. Ought To Fight For Church Schools. . .

17ndezTominational religion was not sufficient; ethics were not enough, but they must go deep down to the faith of the Church as a necessity for avoiding what they now saw abroad. As Churchpeople they ought to go on fighting for Church schools, denominational religion and the faith of the Church for the children of the Church. There was sometimes a feeling that they should surrender the schools, and many of the clergy had to :fight to keep them, but they hoped that the laity would again come to the rescue and help the Church to maintain her schools, and not lose them, for by so doing they would be overtly assisting in the putting of human opinion and ethics in the place of true religion. The Disestablishment of the Welsh Church, the speaker continued, was now a fact, though for the time being its operations would be postponed. Some there were who Might hope that the Act would be repealed, but it would not 'be, and for all practical purposes the Church of England in Wales was disestablished and was to be shamefully disendowed. Still there was comfort in the reflection that it would in the long run turn out to be the best thing for the Church in question; they who trusted in God knew that perfectly well. Good would come out of evil. Great care would have to be taken in Wales to re-establish the Welsh Church on Church lines. At present, according to the Act, there was the power in Wales to call together a representative assembly to hold the property of the Church and thus practically to govern the Church, because those who held the purse very often pulled the strings. The Act left it in the power of the clergy to go on living in the parsonages and performing their duties, but it also provided that all the clergy should be able to hand over their rights and to receive a com- muted income from a new body which would be established. It was the establishment of that body which was so very important. The mem- bers of it might be nominal Churchmen or they might be what were called "laymen undefined.” A Self-Governing Welsh Church. The House of Convocation at Canterbury would no longer be the governing body for Wales, and in matters spiritual the Welsh people would have to set up their own govern- ing body. Churchmen in Wales would be able to make their voices heard more than in time past, and looking at the whole thing in the right way there were many advantages to be gained in being free from the power of the State. For one thing, as was to be seen in the self-governing colonies of the British Empire, the Church, being disestablished, had a freedom which it did not at present enjoy, and could adapt itself to the needs and conditions of the times more than when it was bound in fetters to the State. So that, although there was a black side to disestablishment, there was also a bright one. They were putting aside many con- troversial matters for the present, continued the speaker, but he could not help thinking that the fact of Russia being Britain's ally in the present terrible war would be very helpful as time went on, helpful in educating this country in true church principles, for the Russian State and Church were the most religious State and Church in the world at the present time. The Russians themselves were most devoted to re- ligion; they were really devout and earnest, and realised, very often more than the people of this country, the sanctity of religion. This nation, continued the speaker, had to be rather wary as regards certafn tendencies noticeable in the re- ligious life\ of to-day. He thought that some- times a little too much attention was paid to externals in worship, and that, perhaps, too gs much money was spent upon those externals effort, drttofoo rliitntlsetaunepenn The u erßtshsiinan s, missionary were never guilty of that, and there was another admirable feature of Russian life, and it was that wher- ever they went they took their religion with them at all costs. The Religious Russian.

All the Russians were communicants—com- municants after great preparation In this country the people needed to get back to the 1 real thing—the coMmunion as well as the wor- ship. He could not help feeling that, PerhaPB' the English had not taken sufficient care in de- veloping regular communion, too often satisfied with t„ he congregational form in of worship. Re w youth quite certain that they needed ' their own Church to pay and were rather more atten- tion to the real essence of the sacramental system- It was a shame to think that our s were allowed to grow up so long without being confirmed and receiving the Sacrament. Many points were brought before the nation as the war went on. The young fellows who were going out to the war and were ready to lay down/ their lives for the nation must not be for- gotten. Perhaps, when they came back home again, having tasted victory and learned how to use arms, they would become a great danger " r in England unless, somehow, they could be won back to true eligion again. As one of the Church papers had remarked, We do not know how long the war will go on, but what we do i know is that there s a great want of true re- ligion amongst the manhood we do want to of the country, and o i a o do s to try and infuse new r eligious life into that manhood." The future of the world depended upon what Eng- lish people did in the near future. Mr. W. L. Raw, the organising secretary of otfb e Lsapne caa sk ehri,r e, whichp ro n I,o_saesd s e ea 0 nvdoetde by the English Church Union for Manchester and thethanks to the Chairman, and carried with acclamation.

BRITISH

A CHANGE OF TUNE.

Truth, like constantly dropping I water, will work its way through. It will even reach the intelligence of the German people, and, maybe, that of the KAISER himself. It was Understood in Germany that her great machine devised for massacring nationalities would roll its way to Paris, then return and crush the Russians, a final roll being reserved for Great Britain. The machine progressed amazingly and Berlin rejoiced exceedingly.- But the tide turned and the Germans have been on the defensive for three weeks, with Paris a far-off dream and the near return to the threatened Fatherland an ever-present and all-depressing night- mare. As we say, the truth is even being recognised at home, and we have the great German paper, ".Berliner Tageblatt," preparing the public for bad news. "The German people," we are told, "await anxiously and with confidence further news from the French war theatre." `! Anxious Confi- dence " is good, but it is satisfactory to learn that news of a great French advance against the German flank would. "not unbalance us." Surely this is preparing for a fall?

We know our front line is protected by broad "echelons" which are ready to oppose such an enterprise. Even if fresh French or English forces advance still further in the north-western direction, they will

, . always encounter "echelons" which our commanders have kept back in expectation of such advances.

Excuses like this are the merest non- sense. There is a vast difference be- tween taking Paris in a rush and being dependent upon broad echelons in the rear to save a beaten army from destruction.

As there is a change of attitude to- wards the general prospects of the war, so there is a new attitude respecting the part England is playing in this awful drama. The GERMAN EMPEROR, when he sent forth his murdering, ravaging legions, signed an Order to his officers which said that it was his "Royal and Imperial command" that you concentrate your energies, for the immediate present, upon one single purpose, and that is that you address all your skill and all the valour df my soldiers to exter- minate first the treacherous English and walk over General French's contemptible little Army. Well, this contemptible little army stood up to five times its numbers at Mons, and, as became a lightweight in face of a heavyweight, backed dis- creetly and delivered a blow as oppor- tunity occurred. The result was that the big bully was so exhausted when Paris had been almost reached that he had to turn homeward for repairs. As we have seen, the foe, after two months' hammering, has to be happy in the knowledge that he can find succour to the rear! And what has the "Berliner Tageblatt " to say on the point? This: The influence of the British reinforcements makes itself more and more known. They are efleFtive to impede us, though the re- eults Will not be lasting. W h y should we not admit this 'An old military proverb saYs, Many enemies, much honour." Victory must be ours all the same. If it is gained after difficulties, it is all the more valuable. What, we wonder, will the KAISER say about this paper's audacity? Why not

admit that England can do something when the KAISER has said it is contemp- tible and must be walked over and exterminated. It is rank heresy. We suggest that the editor should be•placed in Louvain when the next German bombardment of that dead city takes place. Our German contemporary hopes for the best and foretells success, but for the present admits that the "obstinacy of the French attack, which always returns and advances against us, deserves just appreciation and will have taught a lesson to all who ex- pected an easy time."

We must not deny even our enemies the consolation of hope, however. ill- founded ; but they can scarcely believe in these hopes, seeing that the process of attrition unonwhtch LORD KITCHENER counted has seriously begun. The KAISER'S Army set about to carry out his order. They came against our men in formations more dense than the formations of a century ago, and these, the picked young men for the invasion, who were to clear the way for the army of occupation, were shot down like rabbits and were themselyes "exter- minated." Meanwhile our reinforce- ments are making their " influence" increasingly felt. India and the Colanies are sending us some of the finest fighting material the world can produce. Russia has brushed aside Austria; and Germany stands in imminent danger without a friend— save the lovable Turk—abhorred by mankind and damned before Heaven as the author of crimes that will stand as the blackest in recorded history.

""HE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, OCTOBER 3, 1914.

EVERYWHERE,

The average size of in American family is five.

We all admire a hustler. He hasn't Lae to :ell his troubles.

Western Siberia is seven times as large as the German empire.

A lot of trouble isn't so any longer when you stop thinking about it.

The fellow who buries the hatchet may still have a knife up his sleeve.

Some men who won't tell a lie will nod their, heads when one is being told.

No Hustling in Nature.

When Garfield was president of Oberlin Col- lege a farmer brought for entrance as a student his son, for whom he wished a short course. "The boy can never take all that in," said the father. "He wants to get through quicker. Can you arranu it for him?" " Oh, yes," saTa Garfield; "he can take a short course; it all depends on what you want to make of him. When God wants to make an oak He takes a hundred years, but He only takes two months to make a vegetable marrow." Drawing the Line.

He ha-,d worked for the farmer nine years as a hired man, and was apparently contented until his employer added poultry raising to his list of activities. Then the hired man had to write on each egg, with an indelible pencil, the date and the name of the hen that laid the egg. One day the man marched up to his employer and an- nounced: "I'm going to quit." The farmer was astonished. "Why are you going to leave," he asked, "after working for me all these years?" "Well," said the man, stoutly, "I've done pretty near everything about this place now, but I'll starve before I'll go on being secretary to your darned old hens."

Speaking of Home. In the boarding-house parlour they talked of home. "Home," said the old maid school teacher, "is the best place for a married man after business hours." "Home," said the extravagant clerk, "is the dearest and yet the cheapest place for a man to live in." " Home is the flower of which heaven is the fruit," said the young divine. "It's the only spot," said the old bachelor, "where the baby is appreciated." "It's where you are treated best and grumble most," said the widow. "Home," said the landlady grimly, "is the antipodes of the boarding house."

Not Responsible. A North countryman, charged with having set fire to a large hayrick, was defended on the ground t' It he was not altogether responsible for his tions. One of the witnesses testified to the belief that the prisoner was " wrang in his held." _

"Canyon mention any occasion on which the prisoner behaved in a manner to warrant your statements?" he was asked by the learned counsel.

"Yes," answered witness. "Ah knows once at work he got half a crown too much for his wage, and—" " Well," said the counsel, as the witness hesi- tated. _

"He took it back to the manager," concluded witness.

THE PARABLE. There was a rose, a full, delicious bloom Of frailest tint and delicate perfume, Set in rich foliage; 'twas a flower so meek That yet had robbed the blush from goddess cheek.

There. came a wind o'erbold with unchecked

might, That sought to strew its glory in the night; But it withdrew, by friendly winds fore-

warned, 'Neath armoured cloak, well prickle ei and well

• thorned. . And there were buds, well thorned and

prickled, too, With future blooms, just bursting into view; And these had crept still closer, so to aid The rose to brave the wind's marauding raid.

The Lesson. . There was a land, a peaceful, happy land— The whole wide world could shake her out-

stretched hand That sought her friendship and admired her might, And stood for freedom, liberty and right. There was a race, a proud, fair Teuton race, Who cast her friendship back into her face: But, to! her beauty changed to steel and iron, The haughty Eagle faced an angry Lion.

And there were cubs who came from far and wide, To show their teeth at the parental side—

A fearful spectacle that well might awe: The Lion and her young arranged for war! ARTHUR PATRIOT.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0063.txt | included=true | included =====
PEACE IN NOVEMBER,

The Question of Labour Represent- ation.

To the Editor of "The .Examiner."

----- Sir,—May I be allowed to raise a few points, re Mr. Dukes' letter in your issue of the 26th September? While I am an enthusiastic trade unionist and would like to see the Labour movement well represented in every Council and on every com- mittee, yet I must say that if they cannot do better than recent records have shown it would be better to leave matters as they are for a time. Mr. Dukes remarks that you cannot pawn your soul in the Labour movement. Ho W about Jim Larkin? Did not the Labour representation of this country actually sell, not only pawn, their souls on this occasion, with the result he so well knows?

Again, what has the Labour representation done during the present crisis? Would he dare to say that they have done their duty to the people who employ and finance them? Again, there is as much cliquism in the Labour party as there is in any other movement, and in my experience it has always been a power that could be used to good purpose, if properly handled. I am always prepared to take my stand as a trade unionist in the interest of my fellow workers. But it must be in their interest pure and simple. I enclose ray name and address, which the Editor is at liberty to give upon application. THE ROAD MENDER.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0064.txt | included=true | included =====
A MEMBER AND CLIQUES.

To the Editor of "The Examiner." Sir,—Along with many more readers, I was not at all disappointed with the tone of your corre- spondent C. Dukes' reply. It is the, same old style of sarcastic bluff, which we have got so accustomed to, and quite in keep- ing with the past. As "A Member," I made it Plain, I did not object to cliques or private persons wanting to aspire to Council honours if they would pay their own expenses, which is not so in this case, but call upon the poorer comrades to do so. It was also to know where we were standing with the Trades Council, the Independent Labour party, and the Socialist party, but, thanks to the Secretary for the inform'ation, we are led to believe we have been captured by the Socialist party. Where men are found, if this is so, as members of affiliated societies, is it not time we called out "halt" paying for the fur- therance of that party's views? With reference to the "Military Ideal" in the "Labour Gazette" having no bearing on recent developments, why did our local "Jack London" seek to have it reprinted? Surely there was an object? And why were the two hundred and fifty issued called in, and almost three thousand lying undistributed? Surely there is reason for such. In conclusion, the Secretary asks what have I and my comrades to gain, &c. I would reply "Much." Yes, at the present moment, we are asked to increase our fees to increase his wages. Also levy to pay election expenses. Who, I ask, is pulling the strings? I believe it is far more prudent to take per- sonal ambition defeat quietly, than a crushing defeat at the poll under present circumstances. A MEMBER.
