WAR STORIES.

Told In Letters. LETTERS INVITED.

We shall be glad to receive retters for publi- cation, in whole or in part, from those who receive communications from friends or rela- tives at the war. These letters often give, in a few lines, a more vivid description of•,the fighting than can be otherwise obtained; and their publication also informs a large circle of people of their absent friends' whereabouts and condition.

All letters entrusted to us will be eared for and returned in good order.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0066.txt | included=true | included =====
SECOND SOUTH LANCASHIRES AT

i',IONS.

Saved the Situation.

The following are extracts from a letter from Lieut-Colonel Wanliss, commanding 2nd Batta- lion South Lancashire Regiment, with the Brit- ish Expeditionary Force, written after the battle of Mons :

The regiment behaved absolutely magnifi- cently and quite up to its very best traditions, and I cannot tell you how proud I was to com- mand it. We had a very hot time pf it at Mons, especially C and D Companies on the left. Both these companies and the machine gun section lost very severely, and the marvel is that they were not completely wiped out; in fact, had the German infantry fire not been so atrocious, hardly a man could have got away. For some time before we got the order to retire, both C and D Companies' trenches were enfiladed both by infantry and machine gun fire, and their artillery had got on our left flank, and were half a mile in rear of it and enfilading the whole position. On getting the order to retire I told Goren's and Ewart's companies to cover our retreat, which they did splendidly. The machine guns lost 12 men out of 16, and fired up till the very last. The following day our Brigadier told me that our men "had be- haved splendidly," and the Divisional General that "the battalion had saved the situation on the left." Two days later, at Solesmes, the battalion formed the rearguard to the division, and lost seven officers killed and wounded in under an hour and a half, and got away under cover of darkness. In this show I don't think the enemy's guns could have _been more than 1,000 yards off. The spirit and discipline of the men was splendid.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0067.txt | included=true | included =====
A KICKING UIILAN.

South Lancashire Private Loses His

Front Teeth.

Private Goulder, of the South Lancashire Regiment, has been invalided home, after hav- ing had his teeth kicked out by a Uhlan. "Several of us," said Goulder, in an inter- view at Widnes, "got separated from our com- pany at St. Quentin. After tramping about twelve miles we reached a farmhouse. The farmer gave us two chickens, a piece of bacon and some potatoes, and we. were just sitting down to a meal after a long fast when a body of Uhlans came round the corner. We hadn't even time to reath our rifles. They rode straight at us, and one knocked me over. As I got up he turned his horse back and, taking his foot from his stirrup, kicked me in the mouth, dis- placing all my teeth. After I was taken pris- oner I was kicked all over the body. "Just as they were marching us off a patrol of British cavar,lry came on the scene. The Uhlans did not attempt to meet them, but im- mediately rode off. We were greatly relieved at the turn of events, but I was so badly in- jured by the Germans that I was sent to hos- pital."

===== 0002601_19141003_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
FOOTBALLER FOR THE FRONT.

Letter from Former Warrington Forward. •

A. Naylor, the former Warrington forward, wrote the following letter a few days ago to Mr. T. Scattergood, of Buttermarket-street, addressing it from Chel4ea Barracks, London, S.W., where he was then lying with the 4th Battalion of the Grenadier Guards:—

"I must write and thank you very much for the nice present you have sent me. It is just what I wanted, as I have been trying to smoke cigarettes since I have been here, but I can't get on with them. We are all beginning to get used to everything now. lam along with a decent lot of fellows and we are making things as enjoyable as possible under the circumstances. We sent 274 men to the front on Saturday (the 21st ult.), and there's another 300 going on Wed- nesday morning (the 23rd of September), so by the time you get this letter I shall be packing up my kit, and I can tell you that I am longing to get at the Germans, and when I start scrum- maging there will be a move on. Remember 'me to all the boys and tell them that I am as fit as a fiddle and ready to take my place when I get back. I will bring them all some picture post cards and curios from Berlin. I am pleased to see the team is playing so well; it's the first thing I look for in the papers. Thank you again, very much, for the pipe. With King George's permission I shall prize it first." The pipe referred to in the letter was a little present from Naylor's fellow-players and friends in the Football Club.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
ON THE' HIGHFLYER. Crosiield's Ambulance Man in Naval

Duel.

Sergt. H. Flood, one of the Naval Sick Berth Reserve men attached to the Crosfield Division of the St. John Ambulance brigade, has written the following letter to Supt. Rose, dealing prin- cipally with the fact that he was on lI.M. High- flyer when it sank the armed German merchant cruiser :

" I left the rest of the boys in the R.N. Bar- racks at Devonport, and did not get a chance to say good-bye to them. I was , ordered to be aboard this ship at 5 a.m., but it was 5.30 before I got my gear, so I had to do a rush. You will have seen from the papers about our little fight, and I can tell you no more, except my own feelings at my baptism of fire. Every cone of the ship's company was calm and collected, and for myself I could not realise that it was a fight to the death. It was just like an ordinary drill, except that our fire was being returned. Our casualties were only one killed and about a dozen wounded (the papers say five or six), which is absolutely miraculous considering the number of shots that were fired. It was an impressive sight to see a big ship like her catch fire, heel over and then disappear beneath the waves. I shall never forget it as long as I live. We have had a fairly strenuous time so far, this being our first rest since we left England on August 4th. I am pleased to say that I am enjoying very good health, and was only seasick one day. Sorry I have no more news for you at present, but will do my best next time. Re- member me to all in the Lab., and kind regards to Mrs. Rose and yourself.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
WAR AND THE PUBLIC-HOUSES. 2,400 Petitioners for Earlier Closing.

The local petition for the adoption of the Intoxicating Liquor (Temporary Restriction) Act, which would mean the earlier closing of licensed houses, has been signed by 2,400 per- sons, including ministers of all denominations, magistrates, Councillors, and almost every class of citizen. An appeal, signed by the Rector (Canon Willis), Fathee Wilson (rector of St. Mary's), and the Rev. T. T. James ' (president of the Free Church Council), was issued to members of the various churches and chapels. It stated that the advantages of the early closing of public-houses in time of war were obvious to everyone. The petitions will now be handed to the Chief Constable and dealt with by the Licensing Authorities for the borough. The Act was introduced and passed at the request of the Naval and Military authorities. Those who have signed the petition are hopeful that the patriotism of those responsible will be suffi- ciently strong as to induce them to accede to the request of the petitioners.

PASSING WEEK,

MISS CROSFIELD, INTERPRETER.

Miss Truda CroStleld has gone to the Dieppe Hospital to act as nurse and as interpreter. We understand that interpreters are very scarce in France, so that Miss Crosfield's services will be doubly valuable. The Women's Liberal Association, of which she is treasurer, have written expressing their admiration for the action she has taken.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0072.txt | included=true | included =====
A PROSPEROUS FUTURE.

The estimate of how the export trade of the world may be shared among the nations when the war has played havoc with Germany's share of it, and how we shall be able to regain much of the home trade that Germany has lately enjoyed, as stated by Mr. J. J. Bleckly at the annual meeting of Pearson and Knowles, makes pleasant reading for Warrington. The Ger- mans say they are out for victory against France, but for booty—our trade—against us. Many things are happening so as completely to reverse the German plans, and Warrington will do its best to double its trade at the Kaiser's expense.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
MORE VOLUNTEERS WANTED.

We have raised a full Territorial Battalion for foreign service and given thousands—it is difficult to say how many—to the Regular Army. But we want more. The Territorials want a reserve battalion, towards which they already had 300. Towards the remaining 700 they had, last night, secured 187, leaving 513 to be enrolled. Captain Max Woods, command- ing the Depot, Drill Hall, and recruiting officer, will be glad to hear from recruits anxious to serve their country either at home or abroad.

TRAMWAYS IN BUTTERMARKET-STREET. The committees concerned are considering whether in the widening of Buttermarket-street provision shall be made for the laying of tram lines.

CAS QUESTIONS. Councillor Osborne, who, since his advent to active public life, has taken great interest in the administration of the Gas Department, has a long list of questions to ask the Chairman of the Committee at the Council' meeting on Tues- day. The questions cover much ground, and the replies ought to be "enlightening."

BELGIAN REFUGEES:

The whole proceeds of the exhibition at the Star Kinema on Tuesday, amounting to .R 26 have been given by the proprietors to the local Belgian Ilefugee Fund. We understand that refugees may be expected at St. Austin's dur- ing neit week, but if Warrington is not used as a centre the money will be given to the Belgian repatriation fund.

GERMAN BOYCOTT.

"An Old Journalist" writes_:—

0-u-i---soidie;;;Wcieitilors.h-fiVelaid these islands under a debt of gratitude and obligation which it will be quite impossible for the millions in- habiting them ever to discharge. But for their valorous defence and protection our beloved and most fortunate country would have been laid waste by the atrocious German hordes, and we should have shared the sad fate of poor, suffering Belgium. Of course, we have readily pledged ourselves to care for our brave fellows' families and dependents during their absence, and complete arrangements have been made to

do all that is humanlY—p-obie-fOr the wounded; but we stay-at-homes can render material assist- ance in various other ways, one of which is plainly enough indicated in the Warrington I Union's amended advertisement for supplies, re- fusing on any account to accept goods of Ger- man or Austrian origin. Any kind of trading with the enemy is now positively and very properly forbidden under severe pains and penalties, and it goes without saying that no true patriot—only a downright traitor indeed—would attempt any such dealings for the sake of mere money-making. So much for the big firms; but what about individuals, of the gentler sex particularly? They cannot take arms and press to the fighting line, though many of them are encouraging their menfolk to enlist for the preservation of home from violation by the Kaiser's marauding bandits; but they can give substantial aid in the prose- cution of the commercial war which is assuredly destined to powerfully second and consummate the tremendous efforts of the active British ser- vices and their plucky allies. Like the local Guardians who have so promptly set the ex- ample, we must all make up our minds forth- with to have nothing whatever to do with articles of any sort manufactured in either of the countries with which we are at war. As a matter of fact, there is already no lack of evi- dence that thoughtful and enlightened members of the community are fully alive to their patriotic duty and the great possibilities of the situation in this respect. "Marjorie" has made that pretty clear in the woman's column.

IVarrington Footballer Injured. John Waywell, of 88, Folly-lane, the well- known Warrington footballer, is suffering from an injured elbow. He is progressing satisfac- torily.

Women Liberals and the War.

The General Committee of the Lancashire and Cheshire Union of Women's Liberal Associa- tions has decided to cancel the arrangements for holding the annual council meeting at Old- ham on October 19, and recommends that poli- tical,work should be suspended and the organ- isations used as far as possible in assisting schemes of relief during the present distress.

Warning to Street Corner Boys. At a Children's Court at the Police Station on Wednesday afternoon several batches of youths were brought before Messrs. S. Wright (in the chair), J. Upson, and A. J. Jolley, for disorderly conduct in various streets. A nominal fine was inflicted in each case, but the Chairman said that future offenders would be more severely dealt with.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0077.txt | included=true | included =====
A Coming7of-Alle Party.

In celebration of —the coining-of-age of Miss Marion Mills, daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Richard Mills, of 8, Tanners-lane, a numerous company spent a few enjoyable hours together at the Winmarleigh Cafe on Tuesday evening. Music games and dancing were indulged in until mid- night. Miss Mills was the recipient of many handsome presents.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0078.txt | included=true | included =====
Free School of Art Lecture.

An advertisement in to-day's " Examiner " calls attention to a progressive innovation which is being made at the School of Art. The public are invited to attend a lantern lecture to be given at the School next Tuesday evening by the Headmaster, Mr. Mawson, who will speak on "How to understand pictures."

Last NATeek's Weather.

The weather return for the seven days ended September 26th shows a mean temperature of 51 degrees, which is two degrees lower than the average for the preceding week. The maximum temperature was 68 degrees, which was recorded on the 25th ult., whilst the minimum tempera- ture was 34 degrees, registered on aka 20th ult. The general direction of the wind was south- easterly, and the rainfall recorded was only .01 of an inch.

Well-known Lady's Death. A highly-respected member of a well-kw-m Warrington family passed away on Sunday, in the person of Mrs. Isaac Hough, 155, Liverpool- road. Mrs. Hough was 81 years of age and was actively identified with Sankey Bridges Chapel. Her many friends were somewhat surprised to hear of her demise, because it was only a week before that she was amongst them.—The fun- eral took place at Sankey on Wednesday, amidst many manifestations of sorrow and regret.

Drowned in the Fog. At the Stag Inn, Walton, yesterday morning, the Coroner (Mr. E. A. White) held an inquest on the body of James Keith (38), a casual labourer, who resided at 55, Powell-street, and who was drowned shortly before two o'clock on Thursday morning, whilst working for Messrs. Crosfield at their wharf.—The evidence showed that the deceased was backing a boat from No. 1 jetty at Bank Quay, in order to make way for a barge, when, by some means or other, he fell into the water, and, although a life-belt was thrown to him, vanished from sight, the body being recovered a few hours later. It was very foggy at the time.—The inquest was adjourne,d for a week, in order that the Inspector of Fac- tories may be notified.

Horse Impaled in Sankev-street. An exciting incident was witnessed in Sankey street on Monday afternoon when a horse be- longing to Messrs. G. and W. Collins, Ltd., provision merchants, impaled itself on three spikes on the top of the yard gate, which is eight feet high. The animal was tethered to the gate insidefille yard, and was standing quite docile, when aliother horse attached to a wag- gon on the other side of the closed gate com- menced to neigh. As soon as it heard the sound the tethered animal made a leap, topped the gate with its head, and thereby impaled its neck on three three-inch spikes. Dr. Hewitt, veterinary surgeon attended to the horse and had to insert 59 stitches in the neck and head. The animal is progressing as well as can be expected.

Death of Mrs. Wright, of Latchford. We regret to record the death of Mrs. Wright, of St. Mary's-street, Latchford, wife of Mr. F. W. Wright, builder, who passed away on Sun- day at the age of 56 years, after a long illness. The funeral took place on Wednesday at Stretton Church, amid many tokens of respect and affection and was conducted by the Vicar (the Rev. and Hon. C. F. Cross). Among those present at the interment were Mr. F. W. Wright (husband), Mr. Fred Wright, Mr. Jas. Wright, Mr. Lewis Wright Mr. Sydney Wright (sons), Miss Elizabeth Whalley, Mrs. M. Twiss, Mr. and Mrs. L. Whalley, Mr: and Mrs. A. Wright, Mr. and Mrs. J. Bolshaw, Miss E. Whalley, Miss Alice Wright, Mrs. Charles Longshaw, Mr. and Mrs. John Longshaw, Mr. and Mrs. Arthur Wright, Mr. and Mrs. William Miss, Miss Maggie Twiss, Miss Ruth Twiss, Mr. and Mrs. Henry Eyes, Mrs. Thomas Hewitt, Mrs. Patten, and Mr. and Mrs. Storey. . . .

Floral tributes were 'received from the hus- band and sons of the deceased, Mr, and Mrs. Albert Wright and family, Mr. and Mrs. Lewis Whalley, Mrs. Miss and Mrs. Whalley, Miss ,Whalley kßruntwood), Mr. and Mrs. Bolshaw, the Women's Meeting, Mr. and Mrs. Hewitt, Mr. and Mrs. Newton and family (London), Mr. L. Fleming and family, Mr. J. B. Starkey, and the employes._

Mesis. J. Mounfield and Sons carried oat the funeral arrangements.

Wycliffe P.S.A. An open meeting was held last Sunday, when the Rev. T. T. James presided over a good at- tendance. The address was given by the Rev. William Reid, who spoke on "Companionship." It was, he said, known and felt by all that it was not good for man to be alone. It was a sad thing to be alone; it was a dragging-down pro- cess, a breaking-up of the highest in human nature. Companionship, rightly directed, was extremely helpful; it was helpful in that at in- duced in us a sense Of usefulness one to the other. Company of the right kind influenced for good, but company of the wrong kind was a great danger, and it behoved every young man and every young woman, aye, and for the matter of that, every nann and every woman, to see to it that the company they kept was "good and excellent company."—During the afternoon the Secretary read a letter that he had received from Pte. Yeates, who had frequently attended the P.S.A. meetings, and who wrote that he had been in two great conflicts and was wounded at the battle of the Aisne. The meeting decided to send the gallant soldier a letter expressing their sympathy and best wishes of his speedy re- covery, together with the hope that in the days to come he may have the Divine protection Whilst fighting for his King and country. It was also decided to send a sympathetic message to Pte. Yeates's wife.—ln addition to the Revs. James and Reid, Messrs. Wm. Beswick and B. Farrar (secretary) also spoke.—The Latchford and Stockton Heath Massed Choirs attended and delighted those present with sweet renditions of the anthems, "He watched over Israel" and "Sing unto the Lord," and the hymn "Rock of Ages." The choir were under the able leader- ship of Mr. Fletcher. Mr. W. H. Payton pre- sided at the organ.

headquarters,

what the Government gave them. Moreover, they had organisations in the town making gar- ments, &c., for such of the men as might have the bad luck to have to go into hospital. The Warrington Territorials were in a unique posi- tion, and those young men who wanted the best jobs in the Army should come and join up straightaway. (Laughter and applatise.) Some complaints had reached his ears about the allowances to those left behind, an he was pleased to know that the Government were tak- ing steps to effect an improvement in that matter. Only the previous day the Warrington Town Council, having been advised by the secretary of the Relief Committee, decided to petition the Government, stating that they must take the question in hand and do the job completely, so that those left behind need have no fear on the departure of those who kept the home going. (Hear, hear.) In conclusion his Worship said that he hoped that as the result of. that night's meeting the Captain, before he went to bed, would have enrolled the required number of young men. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19141003_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
THE NEED FOR RESERVES.

• Colonel FAIRCLOUGH, who addressed the re- cruits as "comrades," and the remainder of the audience as "friends," said that he hoped before long the young men who had come forward at their country's call would be serving under him as commander of the new battalion; for which position his name had gone forward, and al- though he had not yet been officially appointed, he had every reason to believe that he would be. (Cheers.) In Germany they had a terrible foe to fight—a foe that hated England more than she did any other of the Allies.

Peace Terms Must Be Dictated In Berlin. The people of this country must not allow themselves to be lulled into a sense of false security, but every one of them, old and young, must do what he could to beat the enemy, so that soon this country might be able to dictate their terms of peace in Berlin. (Cheers.) Ani those terms must be such as will ensure that there shall not be another war in Europe either for the present generation or for their children. (Hear, hear.) "What could the young men of Warrington do better than come and enlist in this new battalion?" said the Colonel, drawing to a close. "We want to fight for England and for our women and children, so that they may be spared the horrors that have been witnessed in Belgium." Captain MAX WOODS (the recruiting officer) said he was glad to announce that they had secured getting on for a hundred recruits to form the nucleus of the proposed new batta- lion. Still they required another 600. Their own Territorial regiment was practically ready for service wherever it was called upon to go. Every man in the battalion—and it was over 1,000 strong—was ready and willing, and more than willing, but they must have something behind them, and that was a reserve. He had heard since he came to Warrington that the "Pals" battalion had fallen through. Why could not young men come along and form " Pals " companies? He would put friends to- gether in companies. (Cheers.) The Captain announced that the hours for re- cruiting were from nine to three, and from six to eight in the evening.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
GERMANY-THE IMPLACABLE FOE.

Mr. ROBERT PIERPOINT, who was given a cordial welcome on rising to speak, said it was a pleas- ure to him to know that in addressing that, meeting he was speaking to many of those who were his friends and who had not forgotten him. "You have an implacable foe to face," said the speaker, warming to his subject, "and one who does not give way easily, but if our soldiers are true, as they will be true, to the traditions of their forefathers, they will drive that foe back, and we shall make our peace, not in London or in Paris, but in Berlin." (Cheers.) Never was there a time like the present when the young men were so much needed by the country. They might say "Why don't you do the job yourselves." The answer was obvious! Unfortunately they could not put back the clock and men of his age were no longer useful for the front. (A Voice from the gallery ob- served: " I wish they were !" and cheers were raised when it was seen that interjector was an old man, grey haired, but erect.) What would happen in this country if the enemy had-iiis way? They could easily imagine, judging by what had happened in Belgium. I,et them think of Rheims and then Picture to themselves what the fate of such places as Oxford and Cam- bridge would be—the grand old buildings de- stroyed. Let them also think of St. Paul's and of Westminster Abbey laid in ashes. But he trusted that with the help of their grand Fleet, which was watching, watching day and night—always in imminent danger, but always ready that such dangers as he had hinted at would be, as they had been in the past, absolutely avoided. As a friend re- cently remarked to him, they all knew that their Fleet was magnificent and always ready, but what they did not know was that their Army was so magnificent as was proving to be the case. (Cheers.)

===== 0002601_19141003_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
Our Army Splendid, But Too Small.

But, at the same time, the Army, splendid as it was in its courage, great as it was in its deeds, was much too small. That was why they were there that night appealing to the young men of the loyal town of Warrington to come forward. What greater or nobler thing could a young man do than to offer his services to the country that he loved? (Cheers.) If he might travesty for a moment he would ask:

And how can man die better Than facing fearful odds, ' For the ashes of his fathers And the temples of his Gods?

And for the tender mother Who dangled him to rest, And for the wife who nurses His baby at her breast, And for the holy maidens Who feed the eternal flame, To save Ahem from false William That wrought the deed of shame? (Cheers.)

England at a Crisis.

England, said Lord Roberts, stood at the crisis of her fate. She depended on her young men, and they would not fail her in the hour of her need. (Cheers.) The speaker went on to recall the unveiling of the O'Leary statue, and said that the names of the battles carved thereon would live for ever in the history of the nation. But, to-day, the manhood of the country had the opportunity of emblazoning on the hearts and memories of the nation the names of battles even greater .than those which graced the old flags, battles that would live for ever in the pages of our island story. (Applause.) It might be, the speaker continued, that there were some listening to him who, momentarily, thought, "Why should I forsake my work and my ease? Why should Ibe uncomfortable when others will do the job?" That was not the light in which to look upon the present crisis. When peace came—as they hoped it would before long —and the young men to whom that appeal was being addressed, but who heeded it not, had children clustering round their knees and lisp- ing lips asked, "What did you do, father?" Think what his feelings would be

===== 0002601_19141003_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
)ix-la\-Chapelle, August 19th.

when he answered, "I did nothing in the great war because it was too difficult and too dangerous." On the other hand, think of the pride of the man who was able to say, "I served with the South Lancashires." (Cheers.) "Surely," said Mr. Pierpoint, in conclusion, "you must hear the trumpets sounding, you must hear the drums beating? Listen whilst they call. Don't let your doubts influence you, but go forward like men to serve the King, the kingdom and the Empire.

Thus they answered, hoping, fearing, Some in faith, and doubting some, Till the trumpet voice proclaiming, Said "My chosen people, come." Then the drum, lot was dumb, For the great heart of the nation, Throbbing, answered, "Lord, we come!" (Cheers.)

===== 0002601_19141003_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
PARTY POLITICIANS AN EXTINCT

SPECIES.

Mr. CROSFIELD, who met with a most cordial reception, prefaced a striking address by say- ing that he was very proud to have that oppor- tunity of supporting the appeal that was being made on behalf of what was, beyond all com- parison far the greatest cause that had ever enlisted the enthusiasm, the energy and the patriotism of our people. It gave him special pleasure to find himself standing in the midst of a group of interesting and fortunate survivals of that extinct species, the party politician. (Laughter and applause.) He thought it a very I healthy thing for the country at the present time that party differences had been entirely sunk, submerged, and obliterated owing to the magnitude of the great issue With which we were confronted. (Applause.) He was sure that every one in that hall had rejoiced to hear the really noble appeal that had just been uttered by his old friend, Mr. Pier- point. (Hear, hear.) He felt that hesmust con- gratulate Mr. Pierpoint upon the way the years treated him, for his speech was as worthy of the man, yes, and as full of fire, as, the eloquent and splendid speech—a speech he (the speaker) would never forget—which he made in that very hall on the occasion of the unveiling of the memorial Which they raised to their old friend, Major O'Leary. The war that was raging to-day, Mr. Crosfield continued, constituted the greatest cause that had ever enlisted the enthusiasm and determination of the English race. He did not say that simply because of the vast numbers en- gaged in it, though, undoubtedly, from the spec- tacular point of view, it was not only a terrible, but also a marvellous, and, at times, magnificent spectacle. But it was not that which appealed to them. What was stirring the heart of England as they had never seen it stirred before, and with a unanimity they had never before witnessed, was the deep-rooted feeling they had all got of the overwhelming importance of the issues at stake. He did not say the issue, because there were many issues at stake. They had heard a great deal in some of the magnificent speeches deliv- ered in the country about the first issue that brought things to a crisis so far as Great Britain was concerned. They had heard a great deal about the sanctity of treaties and the rights of the smaller nationalities in Europe to live their lives and work out their civilisation and their salvation along with the larger nations.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
The First Issue.

- That was the first issue that confronted this nation—the question of our duty to Belgium. , (Cheers.) It was about a century and a quarter since our great philosophic statesman Burke, influenced, terrified and rendered indignant as I he was by the excesses of the French Revolution, said that the age of chivalry was dead. They might be permitted to think that that great philosopher erred in saying that. It would be a sad thing for old England now if the age of chivalry was dead. There were other aspects of the present mighty struggle, but so far as the smaller nationalities were concerned there still remained an element of the old spirit of chivalry. Not only in ' the thick of the fight would that be noticed, but when it was all over, and when it mine to a settlement, the first con- sideration among the Allies, great and small alike, would be, "What can we do to make some reparation to the little country that bore the brunt of the agony and with such bravery faced the overwhelming opposition of her brutal foe? (Cheers.) Of course, the fair face of France. too, had been marred and sullied by the brutal invader, and she was, perhaps, the next country for consideration after Belgium. (Hear, hear.) If Britain had allowo the jack-booted, inso- lent, arrogant, pipe-clayed Prussian—(laughter) —to get France under his thumb, this country would have had the next reckoning and all our Empire with it. And the Empire had shown that it knew it. (Cheers.)

—Then there was the 'United States. The schemers of Potsdam had done their level best to hoodwink America, but our cousins across the water • were not having any of it. (Applause.) In fact, as far as he (the speaker) could see, no nation was having it. (Renewed applause.) The other day Mr. Wason, quoting from a letter written by an American friend of his, said that it really appeared that the Kaiser had not got anybody on his side except the Almighty, and they had only got the Kaiser's word for that. (Laughter.) It was not only the interests of the smaller nations, of France and, oft. our Empire, that we were fighting for; there was an even wider cause than that. When this great struggle was over they all knew that-the frenzied competition in armaments which had added hundreds of millions of pounds a year to the national expenditure of the countries of Europe would be dealt with. (Applause.)

What Britain is Helping To Do.

The rulers of Germany were responsible for the whole thing, and by checking this insane and wasteful expenditure Britain would help to inaugurate a new era and would help Europe to take a step in advance. We should help to bring about a healthier condition of things under which the nations of Europe could devote their energies and live their lives to better purpose. But they could not expect to achieve that end unless they made some sacri- fices, and so they were making them, and they were going to continue to make them for the greatest and worthiest object for which sacri- fices were ever made. (Cheers.) For his part, he did not altogether think first of the men who made the sacrifices, but rather of the mother, the wife and the sister, who, staying at home, lived in suspense, reading, with bated breath, the lists that told of the wounded and of the heroes who had sacrificed their lives. Sometimes he could not help feeling that, whilst they ought to recognise that great sacrifices had to be made on the battle field, they ought most of all to respect the heroines who faced those sacrifices and all that they entailed. (Hear, hear.) Also, they ought not to pass over with light recognition the noble work that was being done by the noble women of this country in tending the sick and wounded on the stricken field. (Applause.) Let their respect be first for them! And as for the heroes, who had given of their best, who had sacrificed themselves in limb and even in life, they grieved for them, but at the same time they realised that there was a silver lining to the cloud. They had only to cast their minds around the circle of their acquaintance and they would find that

they all knew some family amongst whose prede- cessors one or more played his part in that world-shaking contest, that mighty battle, the echoes of which seemed even now to reverberate in Europe. Waterloo! Did they know of any event in the whole history of such family that was regarded With so much pride as the fact that a predecessor fought on that historic field? It reminded one of the immortal reputation, the deathless fame, of those people in ancient his- tory whose names,a.nd whose deeds thrilled our nation still. (Applause.) And so he said that, even where some had suffered in the present Titanic contest, there was already a silver lining to the cloudy Next year, on June 18th, they would be celebrating the centenary of the great battle to which he had referred. When that battle took place in 1815 the people of this and other countries had not got their "Late Extras" and "Final Editions" of the evening papers. News was circulated a little differently. When Waterloo was won, one of those who helped to make the glorious news known in Warrington— speaking from a very rudimentary and entirely improvised platform in Sankey-street—was his (the speaker's) grandfather. (Cheers.)

A Glorious Anniversary. The 18th of June next year was the date of the hundredth anniversary of that glorious victory, and he was sanguine enough to hope that by the time next summer came round—and he would not venture to be more sanguine than that-they would not only be celebrating Waterloo, but would also be rejoicing over a triumph that would even put the epoch-making victory of 1815 into the shade. (Applause.) And when the time came for celebrating the success of the Allies' arms in the present colossal conflict he hoped, with all his heart, that many of the gal- lant sons of Warrington would return home with imperishable honour as possessors of a share—the most active possible share—in the achievement of the greatest victory of all time. (Cheers.) Mr. Crosfield proceeded to allude to the race problems and colour problems-wTth which the world would still be confronted. He did not allude to class problems; and sex problems should be handled delicately. 7-I],Tighter.) He did not intend to skate very far over that very thin ice. . (Laughter.) But looking to the future nothing struck him as more difficult or more serious than these race problems and colour problems. Bearing that in mind, he envied the man who was sanguine enough to believe that this war would be an end of wars. Although he dared not hope for so blessed an issue as that, still he was absolutely convinced that a repeti- tion of the present Armageddon—for Armaged- don it was—should he made, so far as the nations of civilised Europe were concerned, now and for ever impossible. (Hear, hear, and ap- plause.) Europe could never afford a second Armageddon, and that was just why they had to make a thorough job of it this time, and why the prospect before the Kaiser and his reckless advisers was, at the present time, as black as Erebus, without one gleam or ray of daylight to encourage them. (Cheers.) He did not speak of an alternative issue to the struggle. This country and her Allies might meet with adver- sity, might have terrible reverses, the war might be prolonged and victory delayed, but it was only a matter of time, a question of months, and victory must rest with Britain and her friends. Victory must be theirs because they could not tolerate any alternative. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Crosfield went on to refer to the miscalcula- tions which had led Germany, through her leaders, into her present hopeless and disastrous position. They thought the gospel of blood and iron was more powerful than the principles of international law and international justice. They did not reckon with that intense love of freedom which pervaded the larger nations of Europe, and was not less intense among the smaller nationalities. They did not reckon with Belgium—(cheers)—and the magnificent courage with which, in the face of overwhelming num- bers, Belgium had resisted a Frutal and cruel foe. Then the Kaiser and his ad- visers counted on the disaffection and 'weakness, as they thought it, of the British Empire. They did not reckon on the Imperial Forces in South Africa being led at the present time by the man who, only thirteen years ago, was this country's greatest opponent in the field. (Loud and prolonged cheers.) They did not reckon on that, because Prussian junker- dom did not understand the healing power and the hidden strength that the concession of self-government in the case of our South African Empire had, in fact, effected. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19141003_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
'OBER 3, 1914.

eat tremendous war an absolute certainty. The first reason was the marvellous and almost world-wide response from the British depen- dencies and colonies. The second reason was that the men were flocking to the colours in their thousands, men who were throwing up situations, leaving home, father, mother, aye,, and in thousands of cases, wives and children, to go for 3s. a day to serve King and country. The third reason was that never was there a time in the history of this country when they could better ask the blessing of the Almighty upon their efforts. Britain had no shame in this matter. Everything she did was done for the cause of righteoufness and justice. (Cheers.) 1 She sought no territory, no aggrandisement. "Don't let it be said of you young men, as you leave the hall to-night," pleaded the speaker by way of conclusion, "that you neglected to do your duty in this great and serious moment." (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19141003_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR TURN NEXT.

Mr. J. ELWELL ha,d a few words to say. If Germany had smashed' France and Belgium, and had been able to humiliate Russia, there could not be the least doubt but that it would have been the turn of Great Britain next. When they saw the ruin that had been wrought in the fair countries of their two splendid Allies, France and Belgium, the people of this country ought to rejoice that they knew not the horrors of war from personal experience. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19141003_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
KEEPING UP WARRINGTON'S TRADITIONS.

Alderman BENNETT then -proposed a liearty vote of thanks to the Mayor for presiding. His Wor- ship, he said, had most ably led his fellow citizens in this great crisis, and they were re- sponding to the calls that had to be made upon them in a noble way. The traditions of War- rington were great traditions, and he did not think the people of the town were falling below them at the present juncture. (Hear, hear.) Right back in the Middle Ages it was written that "Lancashire men like lions laid them about.", Referring to the fact that Wednesday was Lord Roberts's birthday, the speaker said that he believed in the gallant soldier's National Servite propaganda, though admitting that one volunteer was worth two pressed men. " If," the speaker added, "we can fulfil the noble pur- pose which that grand old veteran has at heart, and raise the army we need without ay sem- blance of compulsion, I believe no one will be mcire gratified than Lord Roberts himself. Please Lord Roberts by making that 600 up to- night!" Mr. F. A. FROST seconded, and the vote hav- ing been enthusiastically carried, the meeting closed with the National Anthem, sung with a fervour that it did the heart good to note.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
MEMORIES AS HEIRLOOMS. Mr. Crosfield Addresses the New

Recruits.

A pleasant surprise was given to about 150 recruits for the Territorial (Reserve) Battalion when listening to a lecture in the recreation ground in Bank Park on Thursday morning. Lieutenant Townroe, the lecturer, was referring to the present war, when Mr. Arthur H. Cros- field, accompanied by Mrs. Crosfield and Miss Broadbent, arrived on the scene. Mr. Crosfield briefly addressed the men, re- marking how proud he was that they were amongst the first to flock to the colours to up- hold the name of Warrington and Lancashire. He would like, he said, to repeat what was uttered in the Parr Hall on Tuesday night, viz., that anyone who had the good fortune to go and help in the defeat of the Germans would have priceless memories, which would be handed down to future generations, and would be treasured by those who came after as being something in the nature of family heirlooms.

Continued in next column.

PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

" A Lesson in Temperance" is the title of an original absurdity presented by Solo, a comedian of exceptional merit, at the Hippodrome this week. Many ludicrous and gruesome spectres await the inebriated one, who learns his lesson to the tune of the rollicking laughter of the audience. This is only one of the many good things provided, for Fred Durant and Co. supply plenty of fun in the burlesque " Every- thing." There is plenty of by-play in the sketch to provide mirth, and a smart contortionist turn is provided by one member of the com- pany, whilst the other gentleman "keeps the pot boiling" with his witty sallies. Henry and Leigel present something new in the way of Tango dancing and eccentricity. Mr. Henry is a marvellous forward somersault thrower and acrobat, and thrills the house with his daring somersault over seven men seated on chairs. John and Lilian Grand pay a welcome return visit and their singing in the vocal playlet, "At Home and Abroad," is quite as rich as ever. Edna Latonne is a dashing male impersonator, who scores a pronounced success in her latest numbers. Lilian Carter pleases as a soprano vocalist with a very sweet voice, and the war pictures are full .of interest.

Next Week's Fare.

A first-class programme is announced for next week. It will include George D'Albert, the bright and breezy comedian; and Frank Hart- ley, a clever juggler. Another big turn will be provided by Ernest Montefiore and Co., in "The Little Colonel."

POPULAR DRAMA AT THE ROYAL

COURT.

A drama that makes an irresistible appeal to the emotion holds the boards at the Royal Court Theatre this week, in the shape of that old favourite, "Driven from Home." Aged as the play may be in these days of ephemeral dramatic triumph, it has lost none of its power to enthral—to grip and hold the house from rise to fall of the curtain. Miss Muriel Dean gives a most impressive portrayal of the part of "Laura Raybrook," the persecuted daughter. Mr. George Mitchell, acting with consummate skill, makes the part of the cruel, unbending father almost "live." Mr. T. L. Howe is a dashing Walter Hatherleigh, whilst Mr. G. Gaisford makes a suave-tongued villain. Mr. Alex. Alexandra, as Weetie, the outcast, touches ' the hearts of the audience, and the comedy ele- ment is well supplied by Messrs. Winters, Hardy and Bate (bellringers), Mr. F. C. Imbert as John Anderson, and Miss Belle Tilbury as Margaret Bailey. Mr. Harry Cornwall is very good as the faithful butler. Emotional Drama Next Week.

Miss Leila Zillwood*s Company in the great emotional drama, "The Rival Mothers," will occupy the boards at this popular play-house next week.

STAR KINEMA.

A fine programme submitted on the first three nights of the week included "Nan Good-for- nothing," a delightful film picturing the reform- ation of the somewhat refractory young lady named in the title. FIRST-RATE FARE AT ANDREWS'.

TI4 management of Andrews' Picture House present a strong programme this week. "Alligators" is an instructive natural history film. In "Tony the Greaser" the hero falls in love with the daughter of a ranch owner, but his affections are unrequited. Unaided, t e saves the ranch from an insurrectionist attack, and it is only after lie is found dead on the ranch that the heart-broken girl realises her love for him. "A man for a' that" shows how a young society man becomes disgusted with the insincerity of his surroundings, and, upon the elopement of his fiancee with a fortune- hunting count, marries a girl of the poorer .classes. A humorous film tells how "Bertie and his boots" are not suited to each other, so he takes them off, only to be arrested as a member,of a "barefoot gang" of housebreak- ers. He is, however, rescued from his dilemma by the young lady he adores. " The Woman in Black" tells of a clever crook, who, with her male accomplice, is smartly caught by one of the victims of the precious pair. "When Hazel met the Villain" is the title of a film of strong dramatic interest, telling how there is trouble, because the villain loves the heroine, despite the fact that she is married. His evil plot to kill her, however, fails in a most amus- ing manner, and all ends happily.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FRENCH AT RABAT.

Since the French took a slice of Morocco it has become necessary to transform the old native seaport town of Rabat into a capital city. General Lyautey, the Governor, is now at work on this, and the way he has gone about it is a striking example to city builders. General Lyautey found that the speculators had preceded him and bought up all the best sites for several miles around the old town. They had begun doing as they did at Casa- blanca, which as a result looks as if one had emptied boxes of chessmen, dominoes and dice upon a vast billiard table. Rabat is not to be like this. General Lyautey, on looking over the ground, found this to begin upon : An old Moorish town clustered about an ancient citadel situated at the end of a cape jutting out into the sea. The town surrounded by high stone walls cut by monumental towers with gates in the best style of Moorish architecture. Mosques here and there; a few fountains, and, crowning all, the beautiful Hassbn. Tower, twin sister of the Giralda of Seville. A mile and a half of gardens and orchards, then another Moorish wall.

General Lyautey had architects draw up a general scheme for a city. The old Moorish town is to be left as it is; the new city is to surround it. Among the most striking regula- tions are these: Nothing may be built within a certain zone around the old walls, so that nothing shall hide the imposing grandeur of this Arab wall. Moorish architecture is the only style permitted; all designs for buildings .must be submitted to the Resident-General. The maximum height of buildings is axed by law. In the residential zone the surface built upon may not exceed one-third of the surface occu- pied, the rest being reserved for gardens. The hedges of aloes and cactus have been cut down and wooden fences substituted, thus revealing enchanting masses of orange groves and other fruit trees.

COMFORTS FOR OUR SOLDIERS.

WOMEN LIBERALS' EFFORT.

Mr. and Mrs. Crosfieht at the Memorial

Hall.

. It was indeed an inspiring scene that met the gaze of Mr. and Mrs. Arthur Crosfield when, In Thursday, they visited the "John Crosfield Memorial Hall. Enthusiastic women tind busy machines were turning out articles of lot%irg for soldiers and refugees on a large >cide, re- flecting, in some measure, the determination of the women of the country to do their share— the more womanly and humane share—in the present struggle. Already the Women's Liberal Association have provided about 1,000 garments and they will continue to send consignments of wearing apparel each week. Mr. and Mrs. Crosfield watched the ladies at their work and said a few words of encourage- ment and appreciation to them, hearty cheers rewarding the Liberal candidate and his wife for the trouble they 114 d taken in paying the visit and for the interest they displayed in the work.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
WOULD ENLIST IF SHE WERE A MAN.

Mrs. Crosfield, addressing the workers, said Splendid deeds were being done in that room. She was delighted to see so many working hard to supply garments and comforts to the men who were fighting the battle of the Empire, and, by their wonderful deeds of prowess, were mak- ing a name of which we should always be proud. (Applause.) In war time the lot of the women, who had to stay at home in suspense, was harder than that of the men who had the excitement of the fighting, and then came home to be hon- oured by their fellows and welcomed by their families.

"I wish I were a man," continued Mrs. Cros- field ;"I would enlist straight away. I envy them. (Applause.) The next best thing we can do is to work for them. The women of England are doing quite as much work in their way as the men, because we are working to keep them well and strong, preventing them from catching cold and illness. If the soldiers are fit they will be the better able to defeat the enemy. (Applause.) When this sewing meeting was first started I sent a donation to Mrs. Osborne, thinking it would be large enough to buy materials for months and months, but you have all done such good service that that donation has been exhausted. lan delighted to hear it, and as long as you continue I shall be delighted to send donations. We have sent away a fine supply of garments, and I hope we - shall be able to send them week by week." In conclusion, Mrs. Crosfield said that none of them could be too grateful to Mrs. Min Cros- field for providing them with such charming quarters in which to do their work. (Applauk.) GOOD USE FOR THE HALL.

Mr. Crosfield said he was sure nothing would please Mrs. John Crosfield more than to know that the Hall was being put to such good use so early in its career. It would do her heart good to see it. The lads and lasses of Lanca- shire looked the tragic side of warfare squarely and courageously in the face. They must not forget, however, that their lads were coming home again after having taken part in the greatest triumph which the civilised or uncivil- ised world had ever wen. (Applause.) Having paid a glowing tribute to the organ- isers of the meetings, Mr. Crosfield said that with such leadership the ladies' industry and patriotism, they could not fail to succeed in their good purpose. (Loud applause.)

"FLOW OF GARMENTS." North Sea Mine Sweepers and Belgians

Remembered.

The flow of garments into the Town Hall shows no sign of abatement. On Thursday 488 articles were received and apportioned by Mrs. Edelsten and her helpers, as follows :—Warm shirts, helmets, socks and scarves were des- patched to London for the use of the mine sweepers in the North Sea, on whose behalf the Admiralty have issued an appeal, 129; articles for the use of the French and English Red Cross Hospitals at the front, 288; garments to the Base Hospital at Liverpool, 43; and a parcel of new and second-hand clothing to the Belgian Relief Fund in London. Grateful letters of thanks were read by Mrs. Edelsten from M. Navaux, on behalf of the Bel- gians; the Lady Mayoress of Liverpool, Lady Barr and Mrs. Raw, and from Mr. Stanley Wilson.

ST. JOHN NURSES. A Successful Girl Worker at Appleton.

The ladies 0 the Nursing Division of the Warrington Corps of the St. John Ambulance Brigade are this week sending away their first parcel of shirts, socks, bed jackets, body belts, vests and comforters: They have also a large quantity of old linen for which they have to thank several friends. Worthy of special men- tion is a twelve-year-old worker of Appleton, Amy Mills, who has collected over „t 2 for the fund. "I wish a few more would follow her example," writes Mrs. Fisher, the honorary secretary, "for we need a lot of clothing, espe- cially for the hospitals abroad. Will the people of Appleton accept our thanks for the way they have so nobly responded. Mr. Simpson, an- other inhabitant of Appleton, has collected over £l, and I think for so small a village we have done fine. Our thanks are also due to Mrs. Briggs, who has been an ardent worker for the cause; we should have been lost without her; and Mrs. Williams, of Orford-lane." Thanks are also due to Mr. Armitage, for a large parcel of calico; Miss McClinton, for flan- nel and cotton; old linen from Mrs. Nolland, Mrs. Simpson and Miss Newns; body belts frem Miss Banning; and several friends not known. ===== 0002601_19141003_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

Harvest Festivals - Should these be held as usual now that we are at war? If so, should places of worship be decorated for them? Ought not the proceeds invariably to be handed over to the distress . funds? These questions have been raised, and Xt seems to me they can be simply and satisfac- torily disposed of in a few words. To the first one need hardly hesitate to return an emphatic affirmative. From all quarters we have news of a bountiful harvest, decidedly above the aver- age, as well as assurances of a continued food supply from abroad and the maintenance of our trade routes, with no likelihood of serious in- terruption. In so far, then, as the annual fes- tival is a service of real thanksgiving, it is more than justified by existing circumstances. As to whether emblems of the harvest should be brought into the sanctuary, why not? When I was in Yorkshire I once dropped a line to an old country clergyman asking for a paragraph respecting such an event, and he replied to the effect that the collections amounted to so much, but that he did not allow his church to be "turned into a cabbage show." That indicates where the line might well be drawn on these occasions. There can surely be no objection to the use of flowers and a little fruit at the fes- tivals, though personally I think corn should this time be excluded. The third question is, perhaps, more difficult; indeed, I am not sure that it is not impertinent on the part of an out- sider to pretend to deal with it. Obviously, while a prosperous and flourishing church might feel at liberty to apply the whole of the pro- ceeds to the national or local war fund, a small, struggling one, whose coffers have hitherto been replenished from this reliable source of income ought not to be suspected of selfishness and un- worthy conduct for not doing the like,, especially if they have already had a special collection for the very deserving object in question. The ordinary income of many little churches are bound to suffer during the crisis, and the offi- cials must not be expected to add to their finan- cial embarrassments by sacrificing the proceeds of the harvest festival offertories.

Tobacco for Our Soldiers. I have a good deal of sympathy with the move- ment for providing our brave fellows at the front with tobacco and cigarettes, and should like to commend it if I can do so without ap- pearing to encourage the youngsters at home to premature indulgence in "the weed." I have read the testimony of many old smokers who confidently attribute their freedom from sundry minor diseases to a liberal use of tobacco, the smoke of which they declare is a powerful de- stroyer of germs. I well remember this view being strongly upheld in the "Lancet" some years ago, the chief point of the argument being that the property in the smoke which has this killing effect is formaldehyde, a powerful anti- septic. As a matter of fact, I have the article before me as I write, but must contrive to put the whole in a nutshell. Quite briefly, then, germs are deposited in vast numbers in the nose in the process of inhaling the air, and it is easily conceivable that these are killed by the frequent passage of tobacco smoke through the organ. Formaldehyde is a very powerful disinfectant. One part in ten thousand parts of water will destroy all microbes, while the solution would have no effect whatever on human beings. The frequent presence of a powerful antiseptic in the mouth and nasal passages will presumably be good for the soldiers, particularly in prevail- ing conditions. , I recollect a competition in which a smoker (plainly one of the sort not likely to run into extravagance) kept an eighth of an ounce of tobacco alight for 103 minutes! Whether any imitators of this champion will be found among the recipients of supplies from the local "Tobacco Fund" may well be doubted, 'therefore intending subscribers may give freely with the conviction that the prospective con- signments will be greatly appreciated, and that, as young reporters are fond of phrasing it at the outset of their careers, justice will be done -*slim good things provided. 'Mow Women May Fight. By this time a considerable proportion of my readers have relatives or personal friends en- gaged in or preparing for perilous strife for the integrity and independence of the Empire we all hold so dear, and those who are hopelessly ex- cluded from that particular mode of warfare are naturally asking what they can do, not only directly to assist and comfort these courageous defenders of home and all that it involves and implies, but to cripple the enemy and weaken his rkources. Our women especially feel their fancied helplessness, although some pretty broad hints have already been given in "The Ex- aminer" as to how they may as truly wage suc- cessful warfare against the coarse, brutal, over- bearing and unscrupulous Germans and Aus- trians, as do our allied representatives at the front. With a cunning worthy of the super- sneaks of the universe, the enemy countries' emissaries have managed to flood our home mar- kets with their precious manufactures to the exclusion of British-Made goods, and thus to benefit at our expense to an extent which few of us suspected till the outbreak of hostilities brought us the amazing revelation. They have systematically resorted to the most artful de- vices for the evasion of the Merchandise Marks Act, labelling their articles with innocent-look- ing English names, concealing as far as possible the tell-tale "Made in Germany," and practising other characteristic tricks calculated to deceive the very elect. The United Kingdom seems to be overrun withftheir goods, in comparison.with which English manufactures are so scarce that one wonders what becomes of our home produc- tions. I have done my best to find some in neighbouring towns, and I advise any adven- turous friend engaging in such quest to take onl his voyage of discovery a powerful microscope— that is, if he can get one that has been made in Britain. While I asked for toys, cards, and other things of the kind, only to find that most of them came from the enemy, my wife tried the drapers' shops for gloves and underwear with the like result. On looking for the registered trademark, she almost invariably discovered half hidden among the characters the words " Made in Germany," " Wurtemberg," or "Saxony." Some who were buying these con- fessed to her that they had been quite unaware that Saxony is in Germany. One is glad and proud to learn that several local firms whose f advertisements appear in these columns stock mainly British things. Of course, enlightened 4 inhabitants of these parts have made up their ' minds to stand no nonsense of the kind now Indicated; they are very properly insisting upon guarantees from retailers as recommended in the "women's column" of this paper.

To Co-operators and Others. To paraphrase a famous saying, we are all oostkhe4at trade unionists nowadays, but it is odd ones here and there may have overlooked eoo their obligations in respect to the above matter. While we have all been earnestly denouncing and agitating against sweating in home indus- tries, some of us have been thoughtlessly buy- ing low-Priced German stuff regardless f the fact that the employes of those who have thereby been enabled t British hons 6 have successfully compete against ..,,0.,_ per week at had to work several hours rell'e7wYcouwntryt folk who rates of pay than our who have suffered from our rage for cheap "d nasty foreign lines. More- over, to buy at this juncture anything made in Germany or Austria] at obviously to sup- port an enemy countrys .: the expense of one's own kith and kin, and andtrl,De. false to those who are fighting for us deoabt the bulk of in every possible way. NIGY r on our support .. our retail traders may be they are practical sympathy in this Pencled upon for matter but make elaboratethe of their clearly to a great extent aantdthseeer'n customers. It is for these, then, always for British-made goods, that to ask "gekt them. The big firms may ar- rangements to capture German trade, and iiswdbone trusts they may be very successful; but ever they make their modest purchases, ito,,en- wives would bear these hints in mind and act accordingly, there would be plenty of Work everywhere and little need of distress funds. A complete boycott of all things German wilt do more than anything to hasten the overthrow of the Prussian megalomaniac and his Apaches.

IN THE COURTS.

BOROUGH POLICE COURT.

TUESDAY. Before Councillor H. Roberts (in the chair) and Mr. C. Jackson Holmes.

Knocked Senseless With a Stick.—David Fletcher, a sheet mill worker, 14, Rhodes-street, was charged with assaulting Horace Peers and Thomas Lawton on Saturday, and also with wilfully doing damage to the extent of 155., to the property of Clara Palmer.—The Chief Con- stable said the assault on one of the complain- ants was a most savage one, and as it was ap- parent that the prisoner was not sane at the time, he suggested that he should be committed to the Workhouse on al4 days' order. If it was then found that he was all right he would be brought before the Court to answer the three charges. Dr. McKie was of the opinion that the man was hot always responsible for his actions, and the police shared that opinion.—Prisoner said he was not to blame; he had only acted in self-defence.—The Chief Constable: It was a most savage assault on one of the men whom he knocked senseless with a heavy walking-stick.— Prisoner was committed to the Workhouse on a 14 days' order, as suggested.

THURSDAY.

Before Messrs. W. Roberts (in the chair), A J. Jolley, W. T. Williams and Dr. Naden. Put Her Child Under the Tap.—Alice Louisa Vernon, 3, Ford-street. Warrington, was charged with neglecting her two children.—Mr. Browne, for the N.S.P.C.C., said that the woman and her husband formerly lived in Shrewsbury, and the present proceedings were commenced there. Since then the parties had come to War- rington, to which place the woman belonged. At two o'clock the previous day the woman's nine-months-old child was crying, and she was so vexed with it—being drunk—that she said, " I will drown you or break your temper." She then put the child under the tap, and her mother rescued it by snatching it away from the prisoner, who acted like a mad woman for the time being.—Sergt. Metcalfe said that when he arrested the woman the previous night she was lying on a straw mattress helplessly drunk. When the warrant was read over to her, she said "1 don't neglect or ill-treat them."—Prisoner : I don't neglect them. I do take a little drink now and again, and that is the , cause of the trouble.—The case was adjourned until Tuesday in order that further particulars as to prisoner may be obtained from Shrewsbury. Unattended Horse.—John Arnold, a driver, employed by Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Co., and living at 4, 3lalmesbury-place, was summoned for being at such a distance from his horse and lorry that he had not proper control over it.—P.C. Hawkins deposed to seeing the horse and lorry unattended outside the Roan Cow Inn for 30 minutes. He saw the defend- ant, who said, "Me and my mate have been put- ting beer in the cellar and our people pay for us to stand there."—Cross-examined by Mr. Jenkins, for the defence, witness said the lorry was empty all the time. He could not see down the entry where the barrels were generally taken. He did not see Arnold's assistant come to the horse at intervals.—Mr, Jenkins: Did you notice the old mare? It looked a quiet old soul. —The Chief: We expect that! They all are.— Mr. Jenkins: Did you think it would have taken an earthquake to remove it?—The Chief: , More likely a German shell. (Laughter.)—For the defence it was contended that one of the men with the lorry was near enough to the horse to have control over it.—A fine of 10s. and costs was imposed.

£l,OOO FARM FIRE. Playing with Matches said to be

the Cause.

A disastrous farm fire occurred on Saturday at Woodfall Farm, Stockham, near Preston Brook, which is in the occupation of Mr. Wm. Astbury. The Warrington Fire Brigade were notified of the outbreak shortly after five o'clock and Supt. Turner despatched the steam engine " Major " and a full complement of men under the command of Sergt. Cook. On their arrival they found a large Dutch barn, 40 yards long, and its contents—eight bays of oats, wheat and baled straw—burning fiercely. Two other bays of baled straw in the vicinity were also ablaze. Prior to the arrival of the fire brigade the farm hands had removed a number of cattle from an adjoining shippon, but these voluntary workers were powerless to prevent the flames from spreading. The War- rington Brigade turned their attention to the saving of adjoining farm buildings and produce, but their task was made difficult owing to an in- sufficiency of water, which had to be pumped a distance of over 470 yards. It was fortunate that the wind was blowing the sparks away from other buildings in the vicinity, a fact which materially assisted the brigade in preventing ' the fire from spreading. Throughout the night the brigade were engaged in combating the flames, and it was not until two o'clock on Sun- day afternoon that the engine arrived back at headquarters. The premises are owned by Sir Richard Brooke, but the farmer suffers severely, as only part of the produce was insured. The damage is estimated at over £l,OOO. It is stated that the fire was caused by the son of a teamster playing with matches near the barn.

Summer's Joys. Senator Beveridge, in an after-dinner speech in Indianapolis, said of the summer: "The summer has its delights, but it also has its drawbacks. I heard one woman say to an- other the other day: My ,husband is plain spoken. He calls a spade a spade.' "'So does mine,' said the other woman, 'but I decline to repeat what he calls a lawn mower."

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, OCTOBER 3, 1914.

HARVEST FESTIVALS,

PARISH CHURCH.

The Bishop of Whalley was the preacher at the harvest thanksgiving service in the Parish Church on Thursday evening. There was a large congregation, and the choir rendered Barnby's tuneful anthem, "The Lord of the harvest," in delightful fashion, Mr. F. M. Darby presiding at the organ. Suitable harvest hymns were sung. The Bishop, in the course of his address, re- ferred to the recent controversy as -to whether, in view of the war, they ought to do away with harvest festivals this year. He could not help thinking that they would agree with him that if there ever was a year when it was their bounden duty to offer their most heartfelt thanks to God for the gifts of the earth that He had so boun- tifully bestowed upon them, it was this year be- yond all others. When they thought of some of those countries on the Continent, Belgium and the North of France: in particular, with their cornfields devastated by the ravages of war, or where the corn was uneut because there were none to gather it in, and compared that state' of things with what obtained in England, ought they not to thank God for His blessings? Taking for his text "Thou that hearest the Drayer, unto Thee shall all flesh come" (Psalm lxv. 2), the Bishop spoke eloquently on the power of prayer and said it was wonderful to see what the war had taught in the way of self- sacrifice and unselfishness. But if there was one thing more than another the war had taught them it was the power of prayer. They should all pray for the safety of our soldiers, who were fighting so splendidly for their coun-

try. Incidentally the Bishop mentioned that that night he was privileged to preach in the church of which his grandfather was the rector over 100 years ago. He believed that his grandfather was rector of Warrington for 20 years, beginning his tenure in 1802.

Harvest thanksgiving services were held at the Methodist Free Church, Buckley-street, on Sunday, when Mr. R. Pinches, of Chester, was the special preacher. There was a specially large congregation in the evening, when the choir rendered two anthems, "The Lord is my shepherd" and "We plough the fields and scatter." WORKING MEN'S MISSION, BANK QUAY. " We have our harvest festivals so that we may be brought into closer communion with God, and, recognising the bountifulness of His supply, allow Him to send us back again into the world to serve Him in return." This sen- tence from the evening serdion was the keynote of the thanksgiving services at the Working Men's Mission Church on Sunday, when large congregations assembled to render thanks for a bountiful and safely gathered harvest. The building was nicely decorated with flowers and evergreens, while on the communion table some splendid specimens of the fruits of the earth were exhibited. The preacher was Mr. Win- nard, of the Baptist College, Manchester, who delivered appropriate sermons morning and evening. Special music was rendered by the choir, the anthems at the evening service being " 0 Father, Whose almighty power," and " Break forth into joy," whilst Mr. A. Dale con- tributed a solo, "Nearer, my God, to Thee." A musical service was held in the afternoon. Mr. A. Tilling, jun., presided, and Mr. Winnard gave a short address. Mrs. Winstanley sang very effectively the solos "0 Divine Redeemer" and "The Gentle Shepherd," and the choir ren- dered the anthems "Send out Thy light" and "0 Father, Whose almighty power." Miss E. Tanner sang the part in an arrangement for semi-chorus of "As pants the hart," and Mr. Campbell contributed "Sun of my soul." Millington, Mr. Dut- ton delighting the audience with same capital recitations. The proceeds of the banquet and the sale of fruit and vegetables were sent to the Belgian Relief Fund. GOLBORNE-STREET BAPTIST CHURCH. Harvest thanksgiving services were held last Sunday in the Golborne-street Baptist Church, when the special preacher was the Rev. A. E. Robinson, of %Manchester. The church was tastefully decorated with fruit, plants and flowers, ana most dusnt der the direction of Mrs. H. B pleasing effect was secured. The gifts of fruit were somewhat fewer in number than last year, which was only what was to be expected in the circumstances. Preaching in the morning from the words "The garden of the Lord" (Gen. ii. 8), the Rev. Robinson said that people should always bear in mind the fact that the garden was the Lord's. "God only was the maker of all things bright and fair," a fact which man too often forgot. Without God man could do nothing. He was too prone to calculate on the human factor, which was notoriously uncertain, whereas the greatest factor and the only really reliable one was "the Giver of every good and perfect gift," whom men in these days so commonly ignored. God planted the garden for a purpose and it was that man might cultivate it. Man must till and sow and reap. He must weed and fence and guard, and, in obedience to those Divine laws, he would reap the harvest. The afternoon service was under the chair- manship of Mr. Moss, of Stockton Heath, who, in the course of a few opening remarks, re- minded the Sunday school scholars and teachers that the flowers, in all their beauty, indicated labour. To him the children were the buds in God's fairest garden, which needed and de- manded man's care.—The speaker for the after- noon was the Rev. T. T. James, who reminded • the children that it was impossible for them, their parents and those around them to count their blessings, so manifold were they. In in- teresting fashion the rev, gentleman inculcated the lesson that what God most desires is "our humble, thankful heart." At the evening service the Rev. Robinkon's subject was "The co-operation of God and man." God, he said, did not work by magic. He could attain the results He brought about without man's aid where He so disposed, but that was not His scheme and aim. There could be a har- avfesrte aNsp-ietrhso autt Gaorsndiianpoista.l.Thmearne waswas aadnmairt tme dy to partnership with God. Man was honoured by Divine responsibility. The Lord could con- t entdei ah iws istetumt es.o withoutare y , but u ct aGr eo yd. wou Thusld notdid the Divine and the human work together for the extension of His kingdom. During the day the choir rendered excellent anthems, the solos in which were taken by Miss Bates and Mr. H. Barnes. In the afternoon the pieces rendered were " How excellent? and "Oh, come, let us sing." In the evening "The earth is the Lord's" and "Rejoice with one/ accord" were sung. DICKENS AND SOCIAL REFORM.

The Debt We Owe to the Great Novelist

ADULT SCHOOL LECTURE.

"Dickens and Social Reform"' was the title of a most instructive lecturette given at the Sankey Bridges (Men's) Adult School last Sun- day morning by Mr. Stanley C. Jones. Dickens, the speaker said, was no armchair reformer or theorist. He was educated in the bitter school of experience, receiving therein a finer train- ing than either Eton or Harrow could have given for his life's work. At the early age of nine years Dickens began his working life in a black- ing factory, where he was in touch with abject poverty, and lived a hard, uncared-for life. The experience gained here gave him abundance of material on problems of poverty, blind alley occupations, temptations, and vioe of low city life and evil environment, which, later, he worked into his novels. Dickens, even in early years had great qualities in him. From the ages of 15 to 19 he rose from Attorney's clerk to re- porter ill the Parliamentary Press gallery. In the former capacity and in that of reporter- in the Law Courts he became acquainted with the "laws delays," so ably exposed in "Bleak House," and in the Press gallery he learned something of the shams of party politics. Har- old Begbie, in his latest book, says, "Almost every inhumanity against which Dickens hurled the scorn of his impetuous soul has been abol- ished." This was not true, the lecturer said, although as a result of Dickens' many exposures much has been accomplished; still much re- mains to be done. The Poor Law and the prison system, as depicted in "Our Mutual Friend," "Oliver Twist," and "Pickwick," have gone; child life has been infinitely improved, educa- tion as described in "Nicholas Nickleby " is now happily defunct, but much still remains that is a blot on the fair escutcheon of England. Mr. Jones read an extract from "Bleak House," describing one of London's slums. That slum still exists. The speaker gave a graphic and touching description of it, having had two years' experience of its neighbourhood when a student in London. It is within three minutes' walk of our great Houses of Parliament, too, a fester on our organic social life of which most of our legislators aro totally ignorant. Speaking from practical experience of War- rington slums, the speaker said that while they were not so bad as those in Westminster, there were many incredibly horrible scenes in our midst, places where it was not possible for human lives to develop in anything but the vilest conditions, where even the merest moral- ity was almost impossible, and where the filthy surroundings are of the worst description. Dickens's great quality of sympathy for the down-troddeu and the miserable, together with his great powers of dramatic diction fitted him as a great sociar reformer. His command of in- vective was such that his attacks were directed with such - our and knowledge that they com- pelled publ attention. England to-day is reap- ing the fruits of the efforts put forward, and much of the social legislation which has been placed upon the Statute Book during recent years owes its inspiration to the new spirit in- fused into English political life by Charles Dickens.
