ANGLING.

Warrington Working Men's Association.

N.A. CENTRE.

Angling last week-end was not up to expecta- tions. The weather was on its best behaviour for the sport and the majority of members were disappointed. The only place where a good catch was to be had was the free water at North- wich, lklickle Trafford, and Norton. Chester, both river and canal, was fairly well patronised by members, but sport was off generally.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WAR FUND MATCH.

The amalgamated societies are anticipating a good entry for this affair, and as all entries go to the fund, with an effort on the part of local anglers 300 should at least take part in the con- test. There is already a grand selection of prizes collected, and those who are fortunate to he amongst them will be pleased at the value offered on this occasion. The draw takes place at 2.15 at London Bridge, and the contest starts at three o'clock prompt, whether anglers are at their pegs or not, as the contest cannot be left later on account of weighing in before darkness. Those drawing a blue ticket will go to the right of London Bridge, and those drawing a yellow one go to the left.

MR. MOUNFIELD'S UNIQUE RECORD. I have been asked several times to pay Mr. Mounfield a visit to see the finest specimens in or around Warrington, but put off my visit until last week. Arriving at Mr. Mounfield's home in Stockton Heath, the first thing that occurred to me was, whether I had come to see specimen fish set up, or whether I bad tumbled into a jeweller's shop. On my making inquiries, Mr. Mounfield informed me that the trophies were won by ,him on the cycle track, and angling had only become his premier' sport this last three years. He has been an angler all his life, but other sport was placed first until lately. In the season 1912, which was Mr. Mounfield's best year, he took two of the finest specimens in the district of Warrington, and I should say anglers will have to travel far to get species anywhere near their weight. The first that caught my eye was a magnificent trout of 111 b. 60z., and the other, on a wall opposite, was a I splendid pike of 251 b. Now, to give, readers an idea of the amount of sport he had during 1912 on his first visit a week's fishing provided the following specimens :—First day, pike, 61b.; trout, 11b.; perch, 11b. Second day, pike, 121 b.; Perch and four pike, 191 b. Third day, trout, 21b.; two pike, 71b. Fourth day, trout, 41b.; pike, 411 b. Fifth day, pike, 251 b.; pike, bib.; pike, 41b.; trout, 111 b. 60z.; perch, 11b. Sixth day, pike, 511).; pike, 41b.; trout, 11b. Season 1913-14 was not quite as successful, but lie has caught pike up to 161 b. and trout up to 51b. All the above specimens were taken in Lough' Corrib, in Ireland. Any information required by Warrington anglers regarding the place Mr. Mounfield will he pleased to give. It lies in the West of Ireland and is 35 miles long and seven miles wide, and has a depth in places of 240 ft. He remarked that during rough weather the angler deserves all he gets. Bream have been known to weigh up to 141134 caught in the sal- mon nets. If any angler in future thinks of visiting Ire- land for his holidays, it would be advisable to pay Mr. Mounfield a visit and have a chat with him before going. Mr. Mounfield's best catch in England was at Little Budworth, where he took ten bream scaling exactly 201 b.; but pike fishing is his favourite sport, and no doubt he has put up a fine record. He has been a Northern Angler for the last three years, and the older members delight in having a member of his abilities. May he put us up specimens for many years to come is the wish of his fellow-members ! A. S.

STOCKTON HEATH

APPLETON.

Death Follows An Accident.—Much regret wa3 caused when the news became known on Sun- day of the death of Mr. Joseph Lambert, of Yew Tree Farm, following an accident he met with on the previous Monday. He was one of the oldest farmers on the Arley .Estate his parents and a great number of his relatives having lived on the same estate for a number of genera- tions. Having held the positions of overseer, warden at the Parish Church, and, moreover, having taken a keen interest in the annual fete, the deceased was well known and highly esteemed. His familiar figure will be much missed. He was 66 years of age and leaves a widow, two sons and three daughters. The funeral took place at Budworth Parish Church on Wednesday. Floral tributes were sent by tile following: Widow and family, "Annie and Harry," Mrs. W. Lambert and family, Mr. and ,Mrs. Jesse Norcott and family, Mr. and Mrs. W. Norcott and family, Mrs. E. Bower and family, Mr. and Mrs. Struthers and family, Mr. and Mrs. Norbury and family, Mr. and Mrs. E. Britch. The funeral arrange- ments were carried out by Mr. Jonas Hindley. PAD GATE.

Wesley Guild Welcome New Minister.—The opening social of the season in connection with the Wesley Guild was held on Monday evening, when incorporated with the gathering was a re- ception to the Rev. A. J. and Mrs. Morton, Alderman Bennett, who occupied the chair, gave an official welcome to Mr. and Mrs. Morton on behalf of the trustees, and Mr. Duckworth spoke on behalf of the stewards. Both made very touching references to the late Mr. John Bennett and the good work he had done for -Methodism in Padgate and throughout the Warrington cir- cuit. Mr. Morton, in a short, but effective speech, said he was pleased to see such a strong, active Guild at Padgate. During the short time he had been connected with the church he had found that they had many good points, and he : was very hopeful that much good would be done during the three years he hoped to work amongst them.—A very enthusiastic reception was given to the Vicar of the parish, who was present at the meeting, and in the course of a. short ad- dress the rev, gentleman said it gave him much pleasure to attend, as he was glad of the oppor- tunity of showing the members how willing he was to work with the head -of the Wesleyan church at Padgate. If he could do anything to keep up the good feeling between the two churches he would do so to the best of his ability.—Refreshments were provided, after which songs were sung by the Misses Greenough, L. Duckworth, and Mr. Angus, and a duet .by Miss L. Duckworth and Mr. Duckworth, whilst a pianoforte solo was given by Miss P. London. Games, &c., were afterwards indulged in, and a very enjoyable evening was spent by all present.

PENKETH.

Nearly a Centenarian.—By the death of Mrs. Ann Busill, of 11, Warrington-road, which sad event occurred on Tuesday morning, Eenketli has lost its oldest inhabitant. Born at Liver- pool on November 12th, 1821, the deceased lady would have reached her ninety-third year next month. A strong personality, very conservative in her views, and possessing a clear memory, the old lady lived an active life up to the last. An accident, resulting in a broken thigh bone, befel her on the 15th of last month, a shock to the system, from which she never recovered, following. An inquest was held on Thursday, when a verdict of "Accidental death " was re- turned. The interment will take place this (Saturday) afternoon at three o'clock, in St. Mary's Churchyard, Great Sankey. Penketh had been the deceased's home for the last fifty years, during forty of which she lived in the house where her demise occurred. In 1842 she married Samuel BUsill, a skipper of various craft belonging to the firm of Messrs. Richard Evans and Co., who subsequently became master of the steam packet Mersey, a post he held until about 1890, when be retired. Ile died in the year 1900. A sister of the deceased, a Mrs. Johnson, of Bolton, aged eighty-six years, is at present staying in this district as a visitor.

A Few Facts About Fur. "This is cat fur," said a furrier. "We use it for linings. An excellent lining cat fur makes, too. Dogs, calves, colts, coons, opossums, bats, rats, any animal that wears fur, in fact, is sale- able in the fur market. Bat hair is felted up with other stuff into an imitation skin. It is also used, I believe, in rope plaiting. The dog, the coon, and the opossum yield a fur that, pro- perly treated, makes a very handsome lining. Rat skins are employed in certain delicate re- pairs, and they also serve to form the thumbs of cheap gloves. A queer thing about the fur business is that the furs must be taken in the dead of winter; the trapper must work under the cruellest climatic conditions; only thus is the fur at its best. The dresser, on the other hand, who could work best in cold weather, must do all his work in the heat of summer, or otherwise he would not be able to keep up with the changing fashions.

LYMM.

Football.—The Lyinra Wednesday football team will journey to Swinton on Wednesday in order to meet the Pendlebury and Swinton team in a Manchester Wednesday League engage- ment.

For the Belgians.—On Thursday afternoon, by the kindness of Mrs. Ridgway, the children of the village were invited to a magic lantern ex- hibition at the Conservative Club. A collection was made on behalf of the Belgian Relief Fund.

Council Election.—Nominations for candidates for the vacancy caused by the resignation of the Rev. G. a Thurston from the District Countil, will close at noon next Friday. Withdrawals can be made tip to the 15th, and in the event of there being more than one candidate the elec- tion will take place on October 24th.

Belgian Refugees.—A number of Lymm resi- dents have made offers to accommodate unfortu- nate refugees from Belgium. Any further offers should be made to Messrs. W. AluHard and H. T. Mercer, honorary secretaries of the local Relief Fund, or to Mr. T. F. W. Dixon, the honorary treasurer.

Council to Exercise Power of Entry.—At Monday's meeting of the Lymm Urban District Council the members endorsed a resolution sub- mitted by the Water Committee instructing the Clerk to take steps to carry out the power of entry for the purpose of obtaining information as to baths and hose pipes in the different houses in the district.

The Defence Carps.—On Thursday evening the members of the Lymm and District Defence Corps undertook a five-mile route march, which ended at the Cross, where the singing of the National Anthem preceded the order of dis- missal. The men are becoming accustomed to the drilling, but the first route march was re- sponsible for stiff joints the following morning.

Home for Wounded Soldiers.—At Monday evening's meeting of the Lymm Urban District Council a letter was read from Mr. Thomas Grundy, stating that he had offered Brookfield, the house recently occupied by the Misses Mor- ris, as a convalescent home for wounded soldiers returned from the war, and asking that the drains should be tested.—The Council decided to make the test.

Births and Deaths.—Dr. de Jong, the medical officer of health to the Lymm Urban District Council, reported, at the meeting on Monday evening, that the birth-rate for the quarter end- ing June 30 was 24.4 per 1,000 of the population per annum. Thirty-one births were registered, of which 21 were those of males. The death- rate was equal to 9.4 per 1,000 of the population per annum.

Motor and Horse Collide.—An accident oc- curred near Thelwall Station on Thursday after- noon. It is understood that whilst Mr. T. Saville, of Lymm, was driving a motor car, in which was a lady friend, he was run into by a horse. The occupants were both badly shaken, and Dr. Thorp was summoned. The car was badly damaged and was taken away from the scene of the accident yesterday morning.

The Caumeil's Coal.--it was resolved at a recent meeting of the Water Committee that the tender of Messrs. S. Walker and Sons, Lymm, to supply the Council with 300 tons (more or less) of steam slack coal for a term ending 30th June, 1915, at 10s. Bd. per ton, be recommended for acceptance, subject to Messrs. Walker enter- ing into the usual contract.—At their meeting on Monday night the Council endorsed the com- mittee's recommendation.

An Objectionable Practice.—Councillor Holt, on Monday night, drew the attention of the Lymm Urban District Council to what he called "the abominable practice" adopted by certain fishmongers of throwing offal into the streets. "I can give names," he said, "but I don't want to, although if it becomes necessary I am Pre- pared to mention them. I think this thing ought to be stopped." (A Member: " Cer- tainly.")—The Clerk was instructed to deal with the nuisance.

Cost of Guarding the Water Supply.—At the meeting of the Lymm District Council on Mon- day, Councillor Groves raised the question of the cost of guarding the local water supply. He did not think it was fair that the water concern should be charged the whole of the expenses, which amounted to about XlO a month. The ratepayers ought to bear some of the cost, and he moved that half the expenditure be paid by the water concern and the other half by the ratepayers.—Councillor Ingham seconded, and the proposition was carried.

Doing Their Duty.—The following members were present at Monday night's meeting of the Lymm Urban District Council: Messrs. T. H. Warburton (chairman), F. Lockyer (vice-chair- man), J. H. Holt, W. G. Groves, C. E. Cowper, G. H. Parker, F. Ingham, S. Critchley and R. H. Gotobed. The following officials were also in attendance: Messrs. Wm. Mallard (clerk), D. Mort (surveyor), Barnes (water manager), West (gas manager), and W. Timperley and C. Mort (assistants), together with Dr. de Jong (medical officer of health).

Boy Scouts' " Good Turn."—That he shall do a "good turn" every day is one of the rules en- joined upon the Boy Scout. Lately the mem- bers of the Heatley and Lymm troop have been doing several "good turns" daily, for they have been round the parishes named collecting blan- kets for the use of our soldiers at the front, and so effectively have they pleaded the claims of Tommy Atkins that already sixty blankets have been despatched, in bales, to the proper quar- ter. The Scoutmaster (Mr. Nash) is desirous of thanking the donors for the prompt manner in which they responded to the Scouts' appeal. Firemen With the Colours.—TheLymm Urban District Council at their meeting on Monday passed the minutes of the Building and Fire Engine Committee, which contained a resolution approving of the appointment ,of four proba- tioner firemen, as and from August 28, at a re- taining fee at the rate of 30s. per annum, on condition that each man is to be retained only for the period the other firemen are away at the war, and that as soon as those serving with the colours return, the engagement of the tem- porary members of the brigade shall come to an end without notice. The following are the men appointed: Messrs. 11. Daniels, Bridge- water-place; J. W. Downward, Rush Green; John Kemp, the Village; and Edwin Copeland, Pepper-street.

Harvest Festivals.—The harvest festival ser- vices at the Dunham Massey Primitive Metho- dist Church were held on Sunday, when the Rev. R. W. Ferguson, superintendent minister of the circuit, was the preacher. Special anthems were rendered by the choir at both services. The congregations and collections were extremely good. On the following Monday a harvest home social hour was spent, when the fruit, &c., used for decorative purposes was disposed of.—The members of the Lymm Baptist Church also held their harvest thanksgiving services on Sunday. The Pastor, the Rev. E. E. Lovell (who, we are glad to learn, has recovered from his recent . illness), 'preached both morning and evening. Miss Mary Brundrett rendered a couple of solos. The church was very tastefully decorated and good congregations attended each service. Lecturing for the Belgian Relief Fund.— "Children and poets," a lecture delivered by Mrs. Ridgway at the Conservative Club, Lymm, on Wednesday evening, proved to be of a most interesting and instructive character. The pro- ceeds of the lecture were in aid of the Belgian Relief Fund, and it is anticipated that a useful sum will be handed over. The beauty of child- hood, said the lecturer, naturally suggested poetry, in fact, it was poetry ready made, and

summary end just when things looked most promising, a smart piece of tackling by the Oldham back spoiling a nice chance and being the means of sending play rapidly to the other end, where the home defenders found them- selves worsted, Oliver getting over the line in tricky style and Holbrook goaling. From the re-start Oldham again pressed, but at length Warrington managed to break away and to pay a visit to their opponents' territory, but, though they worked hard, they were unable to find any- thing for the scorer to do. A spell of mid- field play ensued, and the game began to show signs of something like monotony, when Har- mer and Mather infused some life into it by uniting in a nice little effort, which, however, like many another promising \move initiated by the homesters; was doomed to failure, the Old- ham defenders proving one too many and much too smart.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0142.txt | included=true | included =====
HOME DEFENDERS HARD PRESSED.

For the next few minutes play was in War- rington's half of the field and the defenders were hard put to it to stave off the determined onrushes of the opposing forwards, but they managed to hold their own for the time being, and, eventually, picking up from ad/scrum, their front line got going in a business-like manner that raised high the hopes of the onlookers. But, alas! those hopes were fated to be speedily dashed to the ground, and Warrington were once more forced to adopt a defensive attitude. For a time they managed to withstand the hot attacks made upon them, but at length, after brilliant play, Smith and Holbrook between them added to Oldham's lead. This additional reverse ,stung the Warrington men to redoubled effort, but they were able- to make little head- way, the Oldhamers holding them at all points. Still, though behind on the score sheet, and obviously up against a proposition too tough for them to solve, Warrington were by no means downhearted, and, like Bruce's spider, they tried and tried again, though unfortunately their trying did not result in any of those tries that are so essential to success on the Rugby field. Holbrook was the next Oldhamer to take the ball over the line, and he "made assur- ance doubly sure" by also adding the goal- points, and hard on the heels of this further rebuff Warrington had to face another, Whar- ton adding still further to the visitors' account. When half-time arrived Warrington were face to face with a debit balance of 18 points, having been unable to register a single point them- selves.

OLDHAM AGGRESSIVE. Almost immediately after the restart Oldham resumed the aggressive and a spirited rush by their forwards came near to further increasing their lead, but some neat tackling on the part of the defenders spoilt the effort just in the nick of time. However, the attackers were not to be denied for long, and a few minutes later found Wharton across the line, Holbrook adding the goal points. Shortly afterwards a free-kick for a foul was awarded against the visitozs. Harold Davies took the kick, but put the ball outside. Just hereabouts the referee found it necessary to have a straight heart-to-heart talk with a couple of the players who had been detected importing rather more heat into their play than the rules governing the game warrant. Oldham continued to have the lion's share of the at- tacking, but the defenders worked hard—and on the whole fairly effectively. However, the wearing down tactics of the Oldhamers at length told their tale, and presently Evans saw an opportunity, and, seizing it, brought off the neatest try of the match. When he embarked on his dash for the line he was well covered by the defenders, but by means of some tricky dodging he managed to elude all the hands stretched out to grasp him and got safely over, amid the plaudits of the crowd, for your real sportsman is as ready to acclaim a smart bit of work when it tells against his side as he is when it tells for it, and your Warringtonian is noth- ing if not "a real sport." When the leather was set going again Warrington showed signs of ' pulling themselves together, and for a time assumed the defensive, but the force of their attacks was soon spent, and once again the Visitors were invading their half of the field in force. Before they were driven to again assume the defensive, however, they. gave the Oldham backs plenty to do, but despite repeated attempts they were unable to get over the line. HOME TEAM PICK UP—ONLY TO RELAPSE ' For the next ten minutes or so Warrington had all they could do to hold the enemy in check, but suddenly the whole aspect of affairs changed, and breaking away,. Laws dashed off at a smart pace, and tricking those opposed to him was over the line almost before the crowd had time to fully realise what had happened. Davis took the kick, but the ball fell short. Heartened by this mead of success, the War- rington lads went to the attack in splendid spirit, and for a few brief moments they were all over their opponents, and it began to look ,as if, after all, they might pull the game out of the fire. Like the players, the crowd also heart- ened and voiced their encouragement after the approved manner. But the homesters' strength was soon spent and once again, having been forced over the half-way line, they were driven to act on the defensive. In front of their own I citadel their strength was repeatedly tried, and this time was found sufficient to repel all at- tacks, and soon the home forwards were again assuming the offensive, and a penalty kick fell to their Ist, Harold Davis putting the ball through in fine fashion. Desultory play ensued, but just before the end it picked up again and became as brisk as the most exacting crowd could desire. At this juncture Warrington were showing their best form, and more than one looked like "bringing off the good thing," but something always went wrong just at the crucial moment. Towards the close, however, their play lost its sting, and Oldham were per- sistently pressing for the last ten minutes or so, the play providing a rare assortment of exciting incidents; indeed, the final moments of the game were, perhaps, the most exhilarating of all, though they yielded nothing tangible in the shape of a score to either side. When the final whistle sounded the score read: Oldham "A," 5 goals 6 tries (28 points); Warrington "A," goal 1 try (5 points).

===== 0002601_19141003_art0143.txt | included=true | included =====
" GAME "__FIGHT.

-q\ftftNa

1i...._

Oldham were undoubtedly the stronger, com- bination, but, on the whole, the Warrington boys put up a game fight in view of the superior odds arranged against them; in fact, they did quite as well as could reasonably be expected, seeing their opponents' record. The home for- wards played pluckily, and those upon whom fell the brunt of the defensive work ..put in all they knew. But the visitors were not to be denied. It may be said that it was not so much faulty play on the part of the homesteris that cost them the match, .but rather the fact that they were outclassed, the opening ten minutes or so of play sufficing to show that they were at a decided disadvantage. Laws played a good, sound game on the half-back line, where he had a useful partner in Douglas. Davis was, per- haps, the pick of the threequarters, and the for- wards were all triers, with little to distinguish any one of them above his fellows. Although the score told so heavily against them, the War- rington men did not lose heart, but fought gamely to the finish, and, though beaten, by no means disgraced themselves. The Oldham for-

wards were a sturdy and tricky lot, and their defensive lines included some sound tacklers.

NORTIIERN UNION TOURISTS HOME

After their successful tour in Australia and New Zealand, the Northern Union football team arrived safely home oh Saturday, merabers of i the Union Committee journeying down to Ply- mouth to welcome them as they stepped ashore after their long voyage. Despite the precau- tions in sailing, rendered necessary owing to the war, the boat Was only 16 hours late. During their tour the team played in eighteen matches and were victorious in no fewer than fifteen of them, the other three being lost. Talking of the tour, the manager (Mr. J. H. Houghton) said that the form of the team had quite come up to expectations, and they had done remarkably well, although the Colonials had spared no effort to place representative sides in the field. Two of the three defeats were sus- tained in the first two games, played when the players had only just stepped off the steamer. The second Test match was lost, hut after a most exciting game England won the rubber. Every match played in New Zealand was won.

Warrington_"A" V. Salford ".A."

-- These teams will meet at Salford, the train leaving the Central Station at 1.50, and the kick-off being timed for 3.30. BRADSHAW'S RE-APPEARANCE. • Bert Bradshaw has been picked as one of the "A" team that will do battle for Warrington against Salford "A" to-day, at Salford. It will be recalled that last year he captained the War- rington team. This is the first time his name has figured in the list of players since the strike of last year. He will play in his old position in the threequarter line.

Rylands' Recs. v. Pendleton. These teams will kick-off at 3.30 p.m. at Gor- sey-lane. The local club will be represented by the following :—F. Horrocks; J. Griffiths, H. Povey, A. Rorrocks and A. Rowlinson ; G. War- ren and J. Gallop; A. Stretford, J. Whittaker, J. Brandwood, J. Broadhurst, F. Potter and E. Smith. The reserve men are T. Riley and J. Dumbell.

ATHLETES WITH THE COLOURS. No Cross Country Running This Season. The Warrington Athletic Club held a special general meeting on Wednesday, with Mr. T. Roberts in the chair, and it was resolved that the spirit of "sport 'as usual" could not be entertained. Therefore, during the continuance of the war, no part will be taken in any cham- pionships or races, but the club headquarters will be open on Tuesdays, Thursdays and Sat- urdays, for physical exercises and instruction on military matters, &c., for all willing to avail themselves of the opportunity. Members, intending members, and their friends are cordi- ally invited to attend. An effort is being made to raise an athletes' section to be included in the Warrington Territorial Reserve Battalion. Those who have already joined will be included. TO-DAY'S FIXTURE. All members are asked to meet at the club headquarters at 2.45 p.m., and to fall in at three o'clock prompt. A capable drill instructor will attend. ITEMS OF INTEREST. The massed run of the St. Helens and district clubs, arranged for to-day has been declared off. The Southern Counties' Association proposes. to run races, &e., as usual. There will not be any "Northern" Champion- ships during, the war. The West Lancashire Association may hold a "Novice Race" this season, but championships are tabooed. The North-Eastern Counties have adjourned indefinitely. Apart from those already serving when war was declared, 30 members of the Warrington A.C. have enlisted, and the present whereabouts of many more is problematical. Will they write to someone? " Jeff " Watson, winner of the. Bank Holiday half-mile, is at Northampton, with "F" Com- pany, 'sth Battalion (Earl of Chester's) Cheshire Regiment. H. J. Lyon is with No. 2 Section of the South Lancashires at Sutton Oak for the present. Of those at the front, "Charlie" Regan has been hit, and "Bert" Tavlin has neck, shoulder and arm wounds. Both are in hospital and are doing as well as can be expected. Harry Smith, who shaped so cleverly in the Bank Holiday boxing exhibition with the Army and Navy champion, writes a very cheery post- card, wishing the lads "the best of luck," and expresses himself still fit to get home some , more rights, lefts and upper-cuts.

• Another Busy Bay for the Juniors.

SOME KEEN AND EXCITING FINISHES.

On Saturday keen and exciting finishes were witnessed in some of the local League matches. St. Barnabas' were the visitors to Hollins Green, and they took a very strong team down, but they had to return with the points divided. The St. Barnabas' boys were cer- tainly a trifle lucky with the goal they scored, but they ought to have registered easily on two other occasions, whilst the homesters deserved a point for the plucky manner in which they tackled their opponents after being in arrears at the interval. Judging by their display on Saturday, not many visiting teams will bring bath points away from the Greenites' ground. Orford White Star had the Cons. from New- ton as their opponents, and a very hard and strenuous game ensued. Neither team could score in the first half, but in the second moiety the Stars began to shine, and they succeeded in scoring three times. The White Star Club have now got together a very good eleven, which includes Denny, Caldwell, Hankinson and Meachin, of Orford St. John's, and Gifford, of Stockton Heath. Fairfield had to acknowledge defeat on their own ground to the Y.M.C.A. team. The home eleven failed to take opportunities offered them in the first half, and in the second the visitors asserted themselves and put two goals on, the homesters failing to reply. The meeting of the Central and Paddington provided a keenly contested game, in which the visitors gained the verdict by the odd goal in five. A kind of local " Soccer Derby" game was witnessed at Latchford between the Baptists and the Elmwood Cons. The struggle was to decide which of the two teams should be the "wooden spoonists" of the League, as neither so far had won a match. The home team had been strengthened for the occasion, and they succeeded in holding the lead at half-time. The second portion was very exciting and some strong feeling was shown on both sides. Even- tually the visitors scored the winning goal about one minute from time. Bank Quay Recs. were visited by the Mission team, and they managed to annex both points, winning by three goals to nil.

WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT LEAGUE. The end of September brings to a close the first lap of the course of the season's pro- gramme, and considering the difficulties which have had to be met, owing to the war, the League members can congratulate themselves on the success which has attended their efforts in being able to keep the League competition in going order. Some keen and interesting games have been witnessed and the players have done much to bring satisfaction to their ispective club committees. Twenty-two matches have been played and there are four undefeated teams—Fairfield United, Paddington United, Longford United, and St. Barnabas'— whilst there are three teams that have not won a match, and of these latter there is one which cannot boast of having ob- tained a single point, viz., Latchford Baptists. There has been some prolific goal scoring, the total number of goals registered being 96, of which the winning teams claim 65 goals and the losers 23, whilst eight goals have been scored in drawn games. The following table shows the present posi- tion of the clubs:—

FAIRFIELD v. EARLESTOWN Y.M.C.A.

The above teams met on Saturday on the Padgate-lane grounds, when the following players turned out : Warburton; Johnson and Jackson; Cartwright, Osborne and E. Roberts; Lawless, Rutter, Clark, H. Challinor and Appleton. Y.M.C.A.: Martin; Atkinson and Pope; Fair- hurst, Gouhlen and Buckley; Taylor, Hughes, Riley, Gilman and Lee. Mr. J Hadkiss was the referee.

The viFitors' captain was successful in the spin of the coin and decided to play with the wind in his favour. The home forwards led off with an attack on the visitors' goal, but their custodian cleared easily, and the visitors carried the game into the home quarters. There they attacked very strongly, but the home defence prevailed. Even play in mid- field followed, both half-back lines proving to be very strong. At length the visitors broke away on the left and Lee hit the upright with a hard drive, which rebounded into play. At the interval neither side had scored.

The opening exchanges in the second half were of an even character, and some keen tack- ling was witnessed, but after five • minutes' play Hughes beat Warburton with a good effort. Nettled by this reverse, the homesters tried to equalise, but the visitors' backs de- fended well, and their goalie was not seriously called upon. The game next turned in favour of the visiting team, but the home defenders drove them back to mid-field, and for a while neither side gained much advantage. As time was drawing near, the combination of the visitors' inside forwards was seen to much ad- vantage, and the home goal was captured a second time, Gilman sending the ball through. From this time onward the visitors had most of the play. Johnson was very prominent among the home defenders for repulsing the attacks made upon his team, and Fairhurst, of the visitors, was the best half-back on the ' field. When the final whistle was sounded the score read :—Earlestown Y.M.C.A., 2 gotta, Fairfield, nil.

WARRINGTON CENTRAL v. PADDINGTON These teams met in Victoria Park, before a fair crowd of spectators. Both clubs wer, well represented, Knight (late of the Adult School) making his first appearance for the visitors in goal. The home team won the toss and tie visitot s set the ball rolling. It was soon evident that the game was to be keenly contested, and some exciting struggles were seen at both ends of the field. The visitors drew first blood after ten minutes' play, following which their goal was hotly attacked. Their defence, however, held out, and their forwards made several futile attempts to increase their lead, when the homesters returned to the attack, and from a capital centre by Craig the scores were made equal, half-time arriving with the score 1-1. On resuming, the game was contested in ding-dong style, and the ball travelled quickly from end to end. At length the visitors gained the lead through a splendid effort by their centre-forward. The reverse roused the home- stars, who at once became very aggressive, and, after Knight had made several good saves, he !had to acknowledge defeat, and the scores wee equal once more. The visitors again got in front by good play on the part of their for- wards, and at the call of time the result was: Paddington, 3 goals; Central, 2 goals.

LATCHFORD BAPTISTS V. ELMWOOD CO NS. The meeting of the above clubs was a very interesting affair, and there was plenty of ex- citement in both camps, as neither team had so far secured a victory this season, and it was well known that the home side were going to make a desperate attempt to gain both points. The following players took the field Baptists: Rushton; Shelmardine and Hardy; Done, Hardy and Albinson; Harding, Pearson, Clark, Stubbs and Blease. Elmwood: Nork; Joe Phcenix and Mellor; E. B. Mellor, James Phcenix and Dutton; W. Phenix, Gleave, Ashton, Wells and Lamb. The referee was Mr. W. Dennett. The Baptists (won the toss and Ashton kicked off before a good crowd of supporters. The home goal was the first to be attacked, but Rushton and his backs defended very well. The home team settled down to their game and the visitors' goal was hotly besieged, with the re- sult that Stubbs scored an easy goal. The homesters continuing to have the best of play, the same player added another point, with a terrific drive which gave Nork no chance. Fol- lowing this the visitors had a greater share of the game, and during an attack on the home goal they were awarded a penalty kick, from which Lamb scored. Even play followed, but no further goals were added in this half, with the result that at the interval the score was: Latchford, 2 goals; Elmwood Cons., 1 goal. The second half opened keenly, each team showing up well and attacking in turn, both goalkeepers being kept busy. The visitors proved the more consistent. A centre from Lamb was only partly intercepted by Shel- mardine, and Ashton gaining possession equal- ised. At this set-back the home team played strenuously to gain the lead, and the visitors' defence was severely tested. However, they managed to clear, and following a spell of mid- field play they returned to the attack, when one of the visitors' defenders conceded a penalty. Stubbs took the kick and the ball hit Nork and went over the bar, the resultant corner- kick being got away. At this stage the visi- tors' half-backs were very prominent, and held their opponents well, as well as supplying their own forwards with good passes. Close upon time a fine centre from W. Phcenix• put the home defenders in a muddle, and Ashton nipped in and placed his team ahead. There was no more scoring before the whistle sounded, leaving the verdict to the visitors. Hardy, at full-back, and Stubbs, in the forward line, were the best of the home team, whilst at centre-half James Phcenix, of the visitors, was at the top of his form. The visitors, as a whole, gave a much-improved display, and they were entitled to the points. Result: Elmwood Cons., 3 goals; Latchford Baptists, 2 goals.

SATURDAY. OCTOBER 3, 1914.

FRIENDLY MATCHES.

GRAPPENHALL v. LEIGH VILLAS.

These teams met on the ground of the first- named on Saturday afternoon., Leigh Villas were without the services of Burrows, Morton taking his. place. Grappenhall started well, and during the first half Ford put on three goals for them, Leigh Villas failing to make any response up to half-time. In the second moiety, however, they did much better, and when the final whistle sounded they were only one goal behind, the score reading: Grappenhall, 5 goals; Leigh Villas, 4 goals. The pick of the visitors were Holden, Doyley and Howells, the last-named playing a fine game in goal, despite the fact that he was beaten on five occasions. With a less competent goalie the adverse balance would have been much larger.

PENKETH FRIENDS' SCHOOL v. EMMANUEL INSTITUTE.

These teams met at Penketh on Saturday afternon in a friendly game. The home team won the toss and kicked off with the breeze in their favour, but they soon discovered that Emmanuel took a lot of holding. After several exciting bits of work in close proximity to the home goal the visitors forced a corner. The resultant kick was well placed, and S. Roberts headed in, thus opening the score for Emmanuel. The visitors continued to have the best of the play, pressing most of the time, and in close succession Eaton and Oakes got through on their behalf, and, as the home team were unable to respond, the score at half-time read: Emmanuel, 3 goals; Penketh, nil. The second half was more evenly contested than the first had been, Penketh showing better form. However, they could not penetrate the visitors' defence. With only a few minutes to go, the visitors increased their score, W. Eaton putting the ball between the uprights as a fit- ting finish to a combined and dashing attack by the whole forward line. This was all the scoring and consequently Emmanuel ran out easy winners by 4 goals to nil.

ITEMS.

St. Barnabas' failed to gather both points at Hollins Green. -

Challinor played a good game at centre-half. Vernon and Houghton missed easy chances of scoring. Atkinson did not get his usual goal for the homesters. Wightman keeps a good goal for Newton Cons. Meachin got two 'very good goals for the White Stars.

Paddington defeated the Central team's intentions.

Knight (late of the Adult School) was good in the visitors' goal. Riley, of the Y.M.C.A., failed to score last week, after having previously brought off the "hat trick" two weeks in succession.

The Y.M.C.A. captured both points on the Fairfield ground. Fairhurst was the best half-back on the field. Johnson and Cartwright did well for the Athletic.

Elmwood have at last gained a victory, Latch- ford Baptists being their victims.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0175.txt | included=true | included =====
The Allies' Line.

On Tuesday Paris abandoned its reticehce as to the precise line that was being held and: contested in Northern France. It was then stated that the Allied Front was as follows:

From Pont-a-Mousson on the east, it passes near Apremont and cuts the Meuse in the region of Saint Mihiel. Thence it turns north-east to Verdun, running from there again almost due east near Varennes and north of Souain to Reims.

From Reims it follows the high road to Berry- -au-Bac. East of this place it crosses to the north bank of the Aisne, and runs along the course of the Aisne to Soissons, chiefly, it appears, on the south bank. Between Soissons and the junction of the Aisne and the Oise it crosses again to the north bank of the Aisne. At this meeting-place of the rivers it turns again almost due north, though with an easterly incline, crosses the Oise, and passes through Cibecourt and Roye to the plateau between Albert and Combles north of the Somme. _ .

On this left flank the enemy's position is also clearly marked. Lassigny and Chaulnes, held by the enemy, face Cibecourt and Roye, where the Allies are posted.

GERMAN TRANSFER OF FORCE. Last week the German right wing was strengthened by the 'transfer of Army Corps, both from the centre of their line and from their left in Lorraine and the Vosges. This force, concentrated near St. Quentin, was at- tacked by the French "operating between the Somme and the Oise." "A general and very vigorous action" followed.

- Along the line of the Aisne, where the British were awaiting their chance, there was little change. Here everything must depend on the result of the flank fighting. On the extreme East, the enemy made some progress among the heights on the east bank of the Meuse, bombarding some of the forts be- tween Verdun and Toul. DESPERATE ATTACKS. - -

Saturday': 13;h:tin; from the front told of fierce engagements on each flank of the battle and of a German advance over the Meuse. This had been driven back by a French counter- attack. The Germans, who had forced the passage of the river, were also threatened by a successful French advance from Toul, which had driven batk the 4th German Army Corps with heavy loss. On Sunday Paris reported " marked progress" on the part of the Allies "on a very extended front between the Oise and the Somme, and to the north of the Somme." _ -

All td-o-ni-the-e-niTenefied line of the centre the enemy had made a number of vigorous attacks, repulsed everywhere except in one part, between Souain and the Argonne, where they made some ground, only to lose it again. "At many places the lines of the French and German trenches are only a few hundred metres apart." It was evident that the Germans were trying at many points to break through, but these desperate attempts to gain ground were checked with heavy losses. .

'On the -Meuse between Verdun and Toul the enemy broke through the French line of forts at St. Mihiel, but the French took steps to see that the enemy did not profit. On Monday the Press Bureau issued a brief summary of the position, showing that on Sun- day night the British lines were attacked with even more vigour than before, but with no more success. The Germans gained no ground. Ear- lier reports from Paris were equally brief, but no less encouraging. At certain points, notably between the Aisne and the Argonne, the enemy had made further violent attacks, which had !wen le pulsed. A Paris bulletin commented on the violence of the German attacks, and described them as manifestly due to an attempt on the part of the enemy to break the lines of the Allies with his collective force, apparently on instructions from the supreme command to achieve a decisive re- sult of the battle. In this the attacks failed..

It is satisfactory to know that fresh British troops have arrived and relieved the men vho had been in the firing line for some time,. The weather has been finer, the nights much colder. "No Man's Land," between the entrenched lines, is, says an official statement, ileaped with the bodies of German infantry, still lying where they have fallen at different times. _ _

The battle Continues to well for the Allied armies. Along the Oise, far above Noyon, the Allied movement against the German right pro- ceeded, extending to the northward as it devel- oped. The report circulated late on Tuesday night that the German right wing had at last been broken and that the Allies were in pur suit remained unconfirmed, but there were evi- dent signs that there was cause for satisfaction at the course of events. The German force which succeeded in estab- lishing itself on the heights of the Meuse near Saint Mihiel had to fight to hold what it r,ained. Ifekvy engagements are reported in this quarter and the Allies advanced at several points, not- abiy to the east of Saint ilihiel.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0176.txt | included=true | included =====
GERMAN SPIES.

Sir John French's descriptive writer explains the German methods of assisting the movements of their armies by the employment of a host of spies, male and female. Men in plain clothes signalling to the German lines by means 9f coloured lights at night and puffs of smoke Ly day; German officers and soldiers in plain clothes or in French or British uniforms left in places that have been evacuated; women spies; secret agents at railheads—are all part of this organised system of secret service.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0177.txt | included=true | included =====
MORE 130MII EXPLOITS.

German aviators were active. in bomb throw- ing during Saturday and Sunday. One killed an old man and injured a little girl in Paris. Attacks of the same kind were made on Belgian towns. A Zeppelin raid on Ostend was attempted last week. The object is now said to have been to destroy trains laden with ammunition. The trucks had been sent away before the airship appeared.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0178.txt | included=true | included =====
RUSSIA ADVANCES

A GREAT ARMY FOR BERLIN AND VIENNA.

It is said that the Czar has at his disposal an army of 5,000,000, with which he intends to hack his way through, to use the eloquent words of the German Chancellor, to Berlin and Vienna.

The German advance from East Prussia has been checked, and it is thought the Germans will find that their great exertions in that region have been thrown away on meeting a Russian fesint to corer the real plan of campaign that is now developing on a large scale. We learn from Petrograd that the attempts by the Germans to cross the Memel near Druss- keniki were thwarted by Russian fire. The German artillery was unable to prevent the offensive movement of Russian troops near Sopotskinie. The retreat of the Germans to- wards the Suwalki Government 'was becoming general. "The fighting in Galicia has been very fierce, says the communique, especially on Ujok Hill, where the Hungarians were dislodged from three positions, and finally retired in disorder. We captured one complete battery, and several hundred prisoners. The pursuit is being con- tipued. AU commuaicAtklite i IPSdi With

===== 0002601_19141003_art0179.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY,

Przemsyl, where no change is reported, though, according to reports of deserters, there is con- siderable disorder among the garrison. The attack on Austria's last hold in Galicia developed the eastern end of the Cracow- Przemysl positions being threatened. At one place it was reported on Monday Cossacks in the Carpathians had crossed the Hungarian border.

Cholera is said to have broken out among the Austro-Hungarian troops. METHODICAL RUSSIA.

Russia is pursuing her campaign methodically and with success. The operations of the Rus- sian armies are now developing in three dis- tinct theatres. One army arrested the German advance from the East Prussian frontier on the River Niemen. The main Russian armies in Galicia are pushing rapidly on to Cracow, leav- ing Przemysl isolated far-in their rear. A third Russian force has broken through the passes of the Carpathians and is invading Hungary and assuming a most menacing character.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0180.txt | included=true | included =====
SOUTH AFRICA. BOTHA TAKES THE FIELD AGAINST GERMANY.

The Expeditionary Force sent by the Union of South Africa, under the command of General Botha,, against German South-West Africa, scored an initial success. It entered Luderitz- bucht without resistance and has hoisted the Union Jack.

The South African force arrived off Luderitz bucht on the evening of September 18th. The surrender of the town was formally demanded and obtained. The occupation then took place. The German garrison had abandoned its defence and retired into the interior, blowing up the railway and destroying the wireless station. Meanwhile the Germans have been active in raids upon the borders of the colony. Walfish Bay, a port held by Great Britain, but sur- rounded by German territory, has been cap- tured. On the eastern frontier of German South-West Africa the police post of Riet- fontein, in the territory of the South African Union, has also fallen to German raiders.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0181.txt | included=true | included =====
GERMAN COLONIES DWINDLING.

Another German colony has fallen to the Aus- tralian Expeditionary Force, under Vice- Admiral Sir George Patey. The town and harbour of Friedrich Wilhelm, the seat of gov- ernment of Kaiser Wilhelm's Land, German New Guinea, has been occupied without opposi- tion. Friedrich Wilhelm has been garrisoned by the Australian troops under the British flag.

SURRENDER OF DUALA. . The following official announcement was made by the Press Bureau :

The operations of His Majesty's naval forces on the West Coast of Africa have resulted in the unconditional surrender of Duals., the capital of the Cameroons, and of Bonaberi to an Anglo French force commanded by Brigadier-General C. M. Dobell, D. 5.0., A.D.C. The Camerobns, which separate British Nigeria from the French Congo, promise a great prosperity. Cocoa, coffee, rubber, kola, and palm oil are all cultivated with success. Cattle rearing in the interior and gold and iron mining will in time add to the country's wealth.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0182.txt | included=true | included =====
JAPANESE CLOSE IN ON TSINGTAU.

The Press Bureau on Monday issued the fol- lowing statement, which was communicated by the Japanese Military Attache:— On the afternoon of the 26th our troops at- tacked the enemy, who were in occupation of advanced positions on the high ground between the Rivers Pai-sha and Li-tsun ; after a slight engagement the enemy were put to flight. On the 27th our troops occupied the line along the right banks of the Li-tsun and Changtsun Rivers, about seven miles north-east of Tsingtau. At dawn on September 28 the Allied Japanese and British forces operating against Tsing- tau began an attack on the enemy's advanced Positions. By noon, in spite of a fierce fire, they had driven the Germans from their posi- tions, and had occupied all the high ground overlooking the main line of defence. The sea- ward approaches were stopped long ago by the Japanese Fleet, which has bombarded two of the forts with effect. When the place falls sev- eral warships will be captured also. At present it is not known what part of the German China Squadron is in the port and what at sea, watch- ing for a chance to raid British commerce. So far only the Emden has proved her liberty.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0183.txt | included=true | included =====
MORE BRITISH SHIPS SUNK.

-- The Secretary of the Admiralty announces that the German cruiser Emden during the past few days has captured and sunk in the Indian Ocean the British steamships Turaeric, King Lud, Riberia, and Foyle, and captured the col- Her Buresk.

The crews of the above vessels were trans- ferred to the steamer Gryfedale, which was also captured, but released in order to take the crews to Colombo, where they arrived on Tues- day morning. Attached to the China squadron when war began, the German cruiser escaped unobserved into Indian waters. Her latest victims are all small vessels, the biggest displacing little more than 4,000 tons.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0184.txt | included=true | included =====
GERMAN LOSSES.

Germany's latest casualty list contains 117,000 names. This does no include losses suffered later than September Ist, and there is no cer- tainty that it is complete for the August fight- ing. During the present month the Kaiser's armies have lost many more thousand men. On September Ist the last stage of the advance towards Paris, the defeat on the Marne, the pursuit northward, and the Aisne engagements had still to come. In the east also the German troops have been fighting hard and suffering severely since the beginning of the month.

NEEDS OF THE CHESHIRES. Advice to Those Who Are Making Gilts.

The appeal written by the Officer Commanding the Cheshire Regiment at the front for socks, shirts, &c., for the men, which was sent to the Duke of Westminster, has resulted in numerous parcels and communications from people having parcels to send being forwarded to Colonel Wil- ford Lloyd at Eaton. The Colonel wishes us to state that he is in correspondence with the Secretary to the War Office, from whom he ex- pects to obtain the precise address to which articles may be sent. In the meantime Colonel Lloyd informs us that parcels for the Cheshires may be sent to Eaton, whence they will be for- warded to their ultimate destination. The par- cels ought to be addressed to The Clerk of the Works, Eaton Hall, Chester, and clearly marked on the outside " FOR THE ,CHESHIRE REGIMENT."

The Appeal. Ist Cheshire Regt., 15th Infantry Brigade, sth Division, 2nd Army, September 16th, 1914. Dear Duke,—Could you use your influence with the people in Cheshire to send the men of this battalion out some socks and shirts? They are hard put to it, as they have only had one pair of socks and one shirt since August 24th. Cheap wooden pipes are a great boon to the men, if you could get some of these sent out too. The Ordnance seem to have failed us in the sock and shirt line.

I wish I had asked you when I saw you in the village of St. Marguerite on the 14th inst., with Colonel Seely.—l am, yours truly, J. LINTORN SHORE, Capt., Commanding Ist Cheshire Regt.

THIRTY-FIVE YEARS ORGANIST. Well-known Catholic Musician Passes Away.

DEATH OF MR. THOMAS MATHER.

Local Roman Catholicism has lost a staunch adherent, the progressive political forces of the town a warm supporter, and many a poor family a kind-hearted friend, by the death of Mr. Thos. father, of Planers-brow, Warrington, who passed away, after a lingering illness, on Friday last.

The deceased gentleman belonged to an old Catholic family, and played a prominent part in the local affairs of the church to which he belonged, and his valued services will be long remembered by his co-religionists. Up to a few years ago, when he resigned on account of fail- ing health, Mr. Mather was organist and choir- master at the Church of St. Mary, Buttermar- ket-street, a dual post he held for nearly thirty years. That his services were appreciated by both the choir and the congregation was attested by the choice testimonial and illuminated ad- dress presented to him by local. Catholics upon his retirement from thelwin-posts named. His energies,. however, were not confined to adding to the beauty of the services of his Church by means of his gift for music, for he served her in many other ways. His connection with the Warrington Catholic Philanthropic Society covered a period of some thirty years, during which he acted both as president and treasurer. He was also, at one time, a prominent figure in the old Catholic Society, and other kindred organisations, now extinct. Keenly interested in politics, nevertheless, Mr. Mather could not be described as a man who followed his party blindly, his independent spirit and clearness of vision preventing him tying himself to any hard and fast political shibboleth. However, his sympathy in Irish politics was with the party of Gladstone, and often, just before his death, he would say that one of the greatest pleasures in his life was the fact that he had lived long enough to see the triumph of Ire- land in her long struggle for Home Rule, a struggle in which he had played no mean part, both in these days, when the cause of Erin, re- ceives more or less sympathetic treatment on all hands, and in the days when to talk of the re-establishment of an Irish Parliament was anathema in the ears of all save the most ad- vanced of Radical politicians.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0195.txt | included=true | included =====
A PROMINENT LIBERAL.

For many years the late Mr: Mather was a prominent member of the Liberal Club, and during the week-end the flag on that building was flown at half-mast as a mark of respect and sympathy. s

Mi. gather was not., only a progressing Catholic, but also a practical one, and he had the privilege of giving two of his daughters as nuns to the Order of the Good Shepherd. Generous of nature, he never lost an opportu- nity of doing all in his power to assist his less happily - circumstanced co - religionists. His death robs Warrington Catholicism of one who will be long remembered by the uprightness of his life and the generosity of his nature. The bereaved family have been the recipients of much sympathy. The mortal remains of the deceased gentle- man were conveyed to St. Alban's Church on Monday night, where they rested until the fol- lowing day, during the morning of which Requiem Mass was sung, the Rev. Father Daw- son, 0.5.8., officiating, and a large number of the general public attending. With Mr. J. Eckersley at the organ the plain Gregorian Chant was sung by a special choir, who were assisted by Fathers B. J. Gibbons, 0.5.8., T. 0. Swarbreck, 0.5.8., and G. C. Jackson, 0.5.8. The interment took place immediately after- wards in the Warrington Cemetery, the Very Rev. Father Whittle, 0.5.8., and Father B. J. Gibbons, 0.5.8. (Leyland, Preston), officiating at the graveside. The principal mourners were: Messrs. Thos. and John Mather (sons), Peter Mather (brother), John Dolan and Edward Rimmer (nephews), Father Thompson (West Kirby), Brother Henry, C.P. (Sutton), James Rimmer and Edward Whitehead (Rainhill), Joseph Dolan, W. Johnson, W. Bate, W. Kilduff, A. B. Marsh, Alban Boyle, G. T. Bibby, Edmund Rigby (Catholic Philanthropic Society), J. Eckersley, A. Kilburn (St. Mary's Choir), J. Richardson, W. Dilworth, Geo. Dolan, H. Woods, A. M. Crowe (United Irish League), Thos. Domville, Houghton and Tyrer (repre- senting the Pawnbrokers' Association, of which the deceased was a member), J. Bate, Goodwin, M. Macdonald, J. W. Bibby, Birchall, H. Houghton, G. Lomax, Henry Rigby, &c.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0196.txt | included=true | included =====
MANY TOKENS OF RESPECT.

The bearers were : Messrs. A. Boyle, W. Kil- duff, J. Eckersley, Edmund Rigby, W. Bate and A. Kilburn. Mr. Jos. Richardson played the Dead March in "Saul" as the funeral cortege left the church. The floral tributes were many and beautiful.

It may be added that the late Mr. Mather was organist at St. Mary's Church for just over a quarter of a century, whilst for nine years before that he officiated in a similar capacity at St. Alban's. It is a noteworthy fact that dur- ing the whole of the thirty-five years that he gave to the musical side of worship in the churches of his faith he was only- absent from his post on six occasions. His uncle, Mr. James Kay, was organist at St. Alban's before him, and between them they occupied the organ loft at the church for a total of 71 years.

===== 0002601_19141003_art0197.txt | included=true | included =====
LOCAL TRAINING CORPS. - Rifle Range To Be Provided. AN AMALGAMATION SCHEME.

Remarkably rapid progress is being made with the Local Training Corps, both in the number of recruits and the general fitness of the men, who carry themselves very smartly. Since it has been decided by delegates from the various training corps to amalgamate under the presi- dency of the Mayor, the desirability of such a step has been seen by all, and the many advan- tages are plainly obvious. The Stockton Heath Corps is amongst those who have joined in the arrangement, and they have decided to hold drills on Mondays, Thursdays and Saturdays. They have been conceded the use of the Council School and playground for the drills. In War- rington it has been decided to form companies of men between the ages of 35 and 50, and other companies for men over 50 years of age, which will be called a veterans' company. A rifle range is being put up for the use of the whole corps, which is at present composed of over 500 men.

APPOINTMENT OF OFFICIALS. A meeting of the newly-formed Executive Committee was held at the Town Hall on Wednesday evening, when delegates from all the various corps were present. Mr. .1. Fulton- Smith was elected chairman of the committee; Mr. Bellian, vice-chairman; Mr. P. H. Bennett, secretary; and Councillor Wilkinson, treasurer. All the local drill instructors were co-opted on the Executive Committee.

Collin reported that Mr. Greening had offered, on behalf of Messrs. Greening's, the use of the rooms at Scotland Bank Works for the purpose of drilling. The offer was accepted with thanks, and it was decided to arrange for the lighting of the room. The further proposals of the committee will be submitted to the whole corps at a meeting to be held in the Parr Hall on October 9th.

TRAINING CORPS AT DRILL. The Warrington Training Corps was well represented at the Arpley Cricket Ground on Saturday afternoon, when, for an hour, the members performed various military drills. Later they went on a route march to Walton and back, through Stockton Heath. The men were under the command of ex-Sergt.-Major Sullivan, and Instructors Stirrup, Prince, An- derton and Lloyd. There will be another drill this afternoon at the Cricket Ground at three o'clock.

First Summons Under New Act. Of Tuesday morning the borough magistrates heard the first local case under the In the Matter of Courts (Emergency Powers) Act, 1914, which has recently been put into operation. The defendant was Peter Hankinson, of 2, Porter-street, who was summoned to show cause why an order should not be granted to Mr. Gittens authorising him to levy a distress for rent.—lt was stated that defendant was in arrears to the extent of £2 is. 6d., and he agreed to liquidate the liability by weekly instalments of 23.-3.14 case WWI adjeurneu ity eignt weeks.

Five shillings will be charged for extra copies of specification. Tenders addressed to the Chairman of the Electricity and Tramways Committee, Town Hall, Warrington, must be sealed with wax and endorsed " Tender for Sub-Station Iron-Clad Switchgear," and delivered not later than 12 o'clock noon on Tuesday, October 13th, 1914. The lowest or any tender will not necessarily be accepted. F. V. L. MATHIAS, Borough Electrical & Tramways Engineer. Howley, Warrington.

had to pass through a darkened room where bags of money were stored. All the men, except the one who danced, had taken money from the bags. A railway in the Argentine Republic has a stretch of 211 miles without a curve or bridge. WHEN DADDY COMES AGAIN. BY JESSIE SIMM. Our house seems very lonely now, For daddy's gone away; ' He's gone to fight for country dear, The foe to keep at bay. "For God's good laws—a sacred cause— We fight," I heard him say. Before the fire his armchair stands, But daddy dear is gone; There is no arm to nestle in, No knee to clamber on. "And I shall not return," said he, "Until the fight is won." But when at length the fight is won, And evil foes are slain, When the great cause of right is saved, He will come home again. And there will be no longing then, No sorrowing, no pain. •:,=,,igfix. = L c .... VA= 12e, an= =ll= r . N , a il Mc Dougalls i N . i '*. Self- Raising' N Flour N , a a II . THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, OCTOBER 3, 1914.

GREAT GERMAN DEFEAT. RUSSIANS ACIIIEVE REMARKABLE VICTORY. Large Captures. The Russian Embassy at Rome has issued a communique stating : The Germans have suffered a terrible defeat in the provinces of Lodz and Suwalki, being I compelled to flee, leaving great quantities of transport and guns. The troops threw away rifles and baggage. Numerous batteries were abandoned in the quagmires. , A Petrograd telegram says the Germans lost 30,000 killed and wounded, and 20,000 prisoners.
